Fine asymptotics for the maximum degree in weighted recursive trees with bounded random weights
Laura Eslava †, Bas Lodewijks ††, Marcel Ortgiese ⋆

Abstract
. A weighted recursive tree is an evolving tree in which vertices are assigned random vertex-weights and new vertices connect to a predecessor with a probability proportional to its weight. Here, we study the maximum degree and near-maximum degrees in weighted recursive trees when the vertex-weights are almost surely bounded and their distribution function satisfies a mild regularity condition near zero. We are able to specify higher-order corrections to the first order growth of the maximum degree established in prior work. The accuracy of the results depends on the behaviour of the weight distribution near the largest possible value and in certain cases we manage to find the corrections up to random order. Additionally, we describe the tail distribution of the maximum degree, the distribution of the number of vertices attaining the maximum degree, and establish asymptotic normality of the number of vertices with near-maximum degree. Our analysis extends the results proved for random recursive trees (where the weights are constant) to the case of random weights. The main technical result shows that the degrees of several uniformly chosen vertices are asymptotically independent with explicit error corrections.



1.
Introduction

The Weighted Recursive Tree model (WRT), first introduced by Borovkov and Vatutin
[
4
]
, is a recursive tree process
(T_{n},n\in\mathbb{N})
and a generalisation of the random recursive tree model. Here we consider a variation, first studied by Hiesmayr and Işlak
[
11
]
, where the first vertex does not necessarily have weight one. Let
(W_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of positive vertex-weights. Initialise the process with the tree
T_{1}
, which consists of the vertex
1
(which denotes the root) and assign vertex-weight
W_{1}
to it. Recursively, at every step
n\geq 2
, we obtain
T_{n}
by adding to
T_{n-1}
the vertex
n
, assigning vertex-weight
W_{n}
to it and connecting
n
to a vertex
i\in[n-1]
, which, conditionally on the vertex-weights
W_{1},\ldots,W_{n-1}
, is selected with a probability proportional to
W_{i}
. In this paper, we consider edges to be directed towards the vertex with the smaller label. We note that allowing every vertex to connect to
m\in\mathbb{N}
many predecessors, each one selected independently, yields the more general Weighted Recursive Graph model (WRG) introduced in
[
15
]
. The focus of this paper is the WRT model in the case when the vertex-weights are
almost surely bounded random variables
.

Lodewijks and Ortgiese
[
15
]
established that, in the case of positive, bounded random vertex-weights, the maximum degree
\Delta_{n}
of the WRG model grows logarithmically and that
\Delta_{n}/\log n\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits^{a.s.}}1/\log\theta_{m}
, where
\theta_{m}:=1+\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]/m
with
\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]
the mean of the vertex-weight distribution and
m\in\mathbb{N}
the out-degree of each vertex. Note that setting
m=1
yields the result for the WRT model. In this paper, we improve this result by describing the higher-order asymptotic behaviour of the maximum degree when the vertex-weights are almost surely bounded. In this case we are able to distinguish several classes of vertex-weight distributions for which different higher-order behaviour can be observed.

Beyond the initial work of Borovkov and Vatutin and also Hiesmayr and Işlak studying the height, depth and size of branches of the WRT model, other properties such as the degree distribution, large and maximum degrees, and weighted profile and height of the tree have been studied. Mailler and Uribe Bravo
[
17
]
, as well as Sénizergues
[
19
]
and Sénizergues and Pain
[
18
]
study the weighted profile and height of the WRT model. Mailler and Uribe Bravo consider random vertex-weights with particular distributions, whereas Sénizergues and Pain allow for a more general model with both sequences of deterministic as well as random weights.

Iyer
[
12
]
and the more general work by Fountoulakis and Iyer
[
8
]
study the degree distribution of a large class of evolving weighted random trees, and Lodewijks and Ortgiese
[
15
]
study the degree distribution of the WRG model. In both cases, the WRT model is a particular example of the models studied and all results prove the existence of an almost sure limiting degree distribution for the empirical degree distribution.

Finally, Lodewijks and Ortgiese
[
15
]
and Lodewijks
[
14
]
study the maximum degree and the labels of the maximum degree vertices of the WRG model for a large range of vertex-weight distributions. In particular, a distinction between distributions with unbounded support and bounded support is observed. In the former case the behaviour and size of the label of the maximum degree is mainly controlled by a balance of vertices being old (i.e. having a small label) and having a large vertex-weight. In the latter case, due to the fact that the vertex-weights are bounded, the behaviour is instead controlled by a balance of vertices being old and having a degree which significantly exceeds their mean degree.

A particular case of the WRT model is the Random Recursive Tree (RRT) model, which is obtained when each vertex-weight equals one almost surely. As a result, techniques used to study the maximum degree in the RRT model can be adapted to analyse the maximum degree in the WRT model. Lodewijks and Ortgiese
[
15
]
demonstrate this by adapting the approach of Devroye and Lu
[
6
]
for proving the almost sure convergence of the rescaled maximum degree in the Directed Acyclic Graphs model (DAG) (the multigraph case of the RRT model) and using it for the analysis of the maximum degree in the WRG model, as discussed above. Hence, we survey the development of the properties of the maximum degree of the RRT model.

Szymańsky was the first to study the maximum degree of the RRT model and proved its convergence of the mean;
\mathbb{E}\left[\Delta_{n}/\log n\right]\to 1/\log 2
. Later, Devroye and Lu
[
6
]
extend this to almost sure convergence and extended this to the DAG model as well. Goh and Schmutz
[
10
]
showed that
\Delta_{n}-\lfloor\log_{2}n\rfloor
converges in distribution along suitable subsequences and identified possible distributions for the limit. Adarrio-Berry and Eslava
[
1
]
provide a precise characterisation of the subsequential limiting distribution of rescaled large degrees in terms of a Poisson point process as well as a central limit theorem result for near-maximum degrees (of order
\log_{2}n-i_{n}
where
i_{n}\to\infty,i_{n}=o(\log n)
). Eslava
[
7
]
extends this to the joint convergence of the degree and depth of high degree vertices.

In this paper we adapt part of the techniques developed by Adarrio-Berry and Eslava in
[
1
]
. They consist of two main components: First, they establish an equivalence between the RRT model and a variation of the Kingman
n
-coalescent and use this to provide a detailed asymptotic description of the tail distribution of the degrees of
k
vertices selected uniformly at random, for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
. This variation of the Kingman
n
-coalescent is a process which starts with
n
trees, each consisting of only a single root. Then, at every step
1
through
n-1
, a pair of roots is selected uniformly at random and independently of this selection, each possibility with probability
1/2
, one of the two roots is connected to the other with a directed edge. This reduces the number of trees by one and, after
n-1
steps, yields a directed tree. It turns out that this directed tree is equal in law to the random recursive tree. In the
n
-coalescent all
n
roots in the initialisation are equal in law and the degrees of the vertices are exchangeable. This allows Adarrio-Berry and Eslava to obtain the degree tail distribution with a precise error rate. Second, this precise tail distribution is used to obtain joint factorial moments of the quantities

(1.1)

\displaystyle X^{(n)}_{i}

\displaystyle:=|\{j\in[n]:\mathcal{Z}_{n}(j)=\lfloor\log_{2}n\rfloor+i\}|,\quad i\in\mathbb{Z},

\displaystyle X^{(n)}_{\geq i}

\displaystyle:=|\{j\in[n]:\mathcal{Z}_{n}(j)\geq\lfloor\log_{2}n\rfloor+i\}|,\quad i\in\mathbb{Z},

where
\mathcal{Z}_{n}(j)
denotes the in-degree of vertex
j
in the tree of size
n
. The joint factorial moments of these
X^{(n)}_{i},X^{(n)}_{i}
are used to identify the limiting distribution of high degrees in the tree. The sub-sequential convergence, as mentioned above, is due to the floor function applied to
\log_{2}n
and the integer-valued in-degrees
\mathcal{Z}_{n}(j)
.

For the WRT model, however, it provides no advantage to construct a ‘weighted’ Kingman
n
-coalescent to obtain precise asymptotic expression for the tail distribution of vertex degrees. As pairs of roots in the Kingman
n
-coalescent are selected uniformly at random and hence the roots are equal in law, it is not necessary to keep track of which roots are selected at what step. In a weighted version of the Kingman
n
-coalescent, pairs of roots would have to be selected with probabilities proportional to their weights, so that it is necessary to record which roots are selected at which step. As a result, a weighted Kingman
n
-coalescent is not (more) useful in analysing the tail distribution of vertex degrees.

Instead, we improve results on the convergence of the empirical degree distribution of the WRT model obtained by Iyer
[
12
]
and Lodewijks and Ortgiese
[
15
]
. We obtain a convergence rate to the limiting degree distribution, the asymptotic empirical degree distribution for degrees
k=k(n)
which diverge with
n
, as well as asymptotic independence of degrees of vertices selected uniformly at random. We combine this with the joint factorial moments of quantities similar to (
1.1
) and use the techniques developed by Adarrio-Berry and Eslava
[
1
]
to derive fine asymptotics of the maximum degree in the WRT model.

Notation.
Throughout the paper we use the following notation: we let
\mathbb{N}:=\{1,2,\ldots\}
be the natural numbers, set
\mathbb{N}_{0}:=\{0,1,\ldots\}
to include zero and let
[t]:=\{i\in\mathbb{N}:i\leq t\}
for any
t\geq 1
. For
x\in\mathbb{R}
, we let
\lceil x\rceil:=\inf\{n\in\mathbb{Z}:n\geq x\}
and
\lfloor x\rfloor:=\sup\{n\in\mathbb{Z}:n\leq x\}
, and for
x\in\mathbb{R},k\in\mathbb{N}
, let
(x)_{k}:=x(x-1)(x-2)\cdots(x-(k-1))
and
(x)^{(k)}:=x(x+1)(x+2)\cdots(x+(k-1))
, and
(x)_{0}=(x)^{(0)}:=1
. For
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
, we write
x\wedge y:=\min\{x,y\}
and
x\vee y:=\max\{x,y\}
. Moreover, for sequences
(a_{n},b_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
b_{n}
is positive for all
n
we say that
a_{n}=o(b_{n}),a_{n}\sim b_{n},a_{n}=\mathcal{O}(b_{n})
if
\lim_{n\to\infty}a_{n}/b_{n}=0,\lim_{n\to\infty}a_{n}/b_{n}=1
and if there exists a constant
C>0
such that
|a_{n}|\leq Cb_{n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, respectively. For random variables
X,(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}},Y
we denote
X_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits^{d}}X,X_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits^{\mathbb{P}}}X
and
X_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits^{a.s.}}X
for convergence in distribution, probability and almost sure convergence of
X_{n}
to
X
, respectively. Also, we write
X_{n}=o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)
if
X_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits^{\mathbb{P}}}0
and
X\preceq Y
if
Y
stochastically dominates
X
. Finally, we use the conditional probability measure
\mathbb{P}_{W}\!\left(\cdot\right):=\mathbb{P}(\,\cdot\,|(W_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}})
and conditional expectation
\mathbb{E}_{W}[\cdot]:=\mathbb{E}\left[\,\cdot\,|(W_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\right]
, where the
(W_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
are the i.i.d. vertex-weights of the WRT model.

2.
Definitions and main results

The weighted recursive tree (WRT) model is a growing random tree model that generalises the random recursive tree (RRT), in which vertices are assigned (random) weights and new vertices connect with existing vertices with a probability proportional to the vertex-weights.

The definition of the WRT model follows the one in
[
11
]
:

Definition 2.1

(Weighted Recursive Tree)
.

Let
(W_{i})_{i\geq 1}
be a sequence of i.i.d. copies of a positive random variable
W
such that
\mathbb{P}\!\left(W>0\right)=1
and set

(2.1)

S_{n}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}W_{i}.

We construct the
weighted recursive tree
as follows:

1)

Initialise the tree with a single vertex
1
, denoted as the root, and assign to the root a vertex-weight
W_{1}
. Denote this tree by
T_{1}
.

2)

For
n\geq 1
, introduce a new vertex
n+1
and assign to it the vertex-weight
W_{n+1}
. Conditionally on
T_{n}
, connect to some
i\in[n]
with probability
W_{i}/S_{n}
. Denote the resulting tree by
T_{n+1}
.

We treat
T_{n}
as a directed tree, where edges are directed from new vertices towards old vertices.

Remark 2.2
.

(i)
Note that the edge connection probabilities are invariant under a rescaling of the vertex-weights. In particular, we may without loss of generality assume for vertex-weight distributions with bounded support that
x_{0}:=\sup\{x\in\mathbb{R}\,|\,\mathbb{P}\!\left(W\leq x\right)<1\}=1
.

(ii)
The model can be adapted to allow for a
random out-degree
: at every step the newly introduced vertex
n+1
connects with
every
vertex
i\in[n]

independently
with a probability equal to
W_{i}/S_{n}
. The results presented below still hold for this model definition as well.

Lodewijks and Ortgiese studied certain properties of the Weighted Recursive Graph (WRG) model in
[
15
]
. This is a more general version of the WRT model that allows every vertex to connect to
m\in\mathbb{N}
vertices when introduced, yielding a multigraph when
m>1
. This paper aims to recover and extend some of these results in the tree case (
m=1
) when the vertex-weights are almost surely bounded, i.e.
x_{0}<\infty
. As stated in Remark
2.2
(i)
, we can set
x_{0}=1
without loss of generality. To formulate the results we need to assume that the distribution of the weights is sufficiently regular, allowing us to control their extreme value behaviour. In certain cases it is more convenient to formulate the assumptions in terms of the distribution of the random variable
(1-W)^{-1}
:

Assumption 2.3

(Vertex-weight distribution)
.

The vertex-weights
W,(W_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
are i.i.d. strictly positive random variables, which satisfy:

(
C
1)

The random variable
W
takes values in
(0,1]
and
x_{0}:=\sup\{x\in\mathbb{R}|\mathbb{P}\!\left(W\leq x\right)<1\}=1
.

(
C
2)

There exist
C,\rho>0,x_{0}\in(0,1)
such that for all
x\in[0,x_{0}]
it holds that
\mathbb{P}\!\left(W\leq x\right)\leq Cx^{\rho}.

Furthermore, the vertex-weights satisfy one of the following conditions:

(
Atom
)

The vertex weights follow a distribution that has an atom at one, i.e. there exists a
q_{0}\in(0,1]
such that
\mathbb{P}\!\left(W=1\right)=q_{0}
. (Note that
q_{0}=1
recovers the RRT model)

(
Weibull
)

The vertex-weights follow a distribution that belongs to the Weibull maximum domain of attraction (MDA). This implies that there exist some
\alpha>1
and a positive function
\ell
which is slowly varying at infinity, such that

(2.2)

\hskip 56.9055pt\mathbb{P}\!\left(W\geq 1-1/x\right)=\mathbb{P}\!\left((1-W)^{-1}\geq x\right)=\ell(x)x^{-(\alpha-1)},\qquad x>0.

(
Gumbel
)

The distribution belongs to the Gumbel maximum domain of attraction (MDA) (and
x_{0}=1
). This implies that there exist sequences
(a_{n},b_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, such that

(2.3)

\frac{\max_{i\in[n]}W_{i}-b_{n}}{a_{n}}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits^{d}}\Lambda,

where
\Lambda
is a Gumbel random variable.

Within this class, we further distinguish the following two sub-classes:

(
RV
)

There exist
a,c_{1},\tau>0
, and
b\in\mathbb{R}
such that

\hskip 42.67912pt\mathbb{P}\!\left(W>1-1/x\right)=\mathbb{P}\!\left((1-W)^{-1}>x\right)\sim ax^{b}\mathrm{e}^{-(x/c_{1})^{\tau}}\quad\mbox{as }x\rightarrow\infty.

(
RaV
)

There exist
a,c_{1}>0,b\in\mathbb{R},
and
\tau>1
such that

\hskip 42.67912pt\mathbb{P}\!\left(W>1-1/x\right)=\mathbb{P}\!\left((1-W)^{-1}>x\right)\sim a(\log x)^{b}\mathrm{e}^{-(\log(x)/c_{1})^{\tau}}\quad\mbox{as }x\rightarrow\infty.

Remark 2.4
.

Condition

(

C

2
)

is required only for a very specific part of the proof of Proposition
5.1
. Though we were unable to omit this assumption, it covers a wide range of vertex-weight distributions. Still, we believe it is a mere technicality that can be overcome.

Throughout, we will write

\mathcal{Z}_{n}(i):=\mbox{ in-degree of vertex }i\mbox{ in }T_{n}.

Working with the in-degree allows us to (in principle) generalise our methods to graphs with random out-degree, as mentioned in Remark
2.2
. Obviously, if the out-degree is fixed, we can recover the results for the degree from our results on the
\mathcal{Z}_{n}(i)
.

In
[
15
]
, the following results are obtained for the WRG model: if we let
\theta_{m}:=1+\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]/m
,

(2.4)

p_{k}(m):=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\theta_{m}-1}{\theta_{m}-1+W}\Big(\frac{W}{\theta_{m}-1+W}\Big)^{k}\right],\ p_{\geq k}(m):=\sum_{j=k}^{\infty}p_{k}(m)=\mathbb{E}\left[\Big(\frac{W}{\theta_{m}-1+W}\Big)^{k}\right],

then almost surely for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
fixed,

(2.5)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\mathcal{Z}_{n}(i)=k\}}=p_{k}(m)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\mathcal{Z}_{n}(i)\geq k\}}=p_{\geq k}(m),

whenever
W
follows a distribution with a finite mean. In particular, the above is satisfied for all cases in Assumption
2.3
. Moreover, if the vertex-weights are bounded almost surely (without loss of generality
x_{0}=1
),

(2.6)

\max_{i\in[n]}\frac{\mathcal{Z}_{n}(i)}{\log_{\theta_{m}}n}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits^{a.s.}}1.

In this paper we improve these results when considering the WRT model with almost surely bounded weights. That is, we consider the case
m=1
. For ease of writing, we let

(2.7)

\theta:=\theta_{1}=1+\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]\text{ and }p_{k}:=p_{k}(1),p_{\geq k}:=p_{\geq k}(1).

First, we are able to extend the result in (
2.5
) to the case when
k=k(n)
diverges with
n
in the sense that the difference between both sides converges to zero in mean, under certain constraints on
k(n)
, and we obtain a convergence rate as well. Combining this result with techniques developed by Addario-Berry and Eslava in
[
1
]
for random recursive trees we are then able to identify the higher-order asymptotic behaviour of the maximum degree depending on the cases in Assumption
2.3
. Additionally, in certain cases we are able to derive an asymptotic tail distribution for the maximum degree and obtain an asymptotic normality result for the number of vertices with ‘near-maximal’ degrees (in certain cases). These results can be extended to the model with a
random out-degree
as mentioned in Remark
2.2
as well.

Recall that
\theta:=1+\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]
and define

(2.8)

\displaystyle X^{(n)}_{i}

\displaystyle:=|\{j\in[n]:\mathcal{Z}_{n}(j)=\lfloor\log_{\theta}n\rfloor+i\}|,

\displaystyle X^{(n)}_{\geq i}

\displaystyle:=|\{j\in[n]:\mathcal{Z}_{n}(j)\geq\lfloor\log_{\theta}n\rfloor+i\}|.

For certain classes of vertex-weight distributions, we can prove the distributional convergence of these quantities along subsequences, as is the case for the RRT model in
[
1
]
. This result can be formulated in terms of convergence of point processes. Let
\mathbb{Z}^{*}:=\mathbb{Z}\cup\{\infty\}
and endow
\mathbb{Z}^{*}
with the metric
d(i,j)=|2^{-i}-2^{-j}|
and
d(i,\infty)=2^{-i}
for any
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}
, so that
[i,\infty]
is a compact set. Following the notation in
[
5
]
, let
\mathcal{M}^{\#}_{\mathbb{Z}^{*}}
be the space of boundedly finite measures on
\mathbb{Z}^{*}
(which in this case corresponds to locally finite measures) equipped with the vague topology. If we let
\mathcal{P}
be a Poisson point process on
\mathbb{R}
with intensity measure
\lambda(\mathrm{d}x):=q_{0}\theta^{-x}\log\theta\,\mathrm{d}x,q_{0}\in(0,1]
, and define

(2.9)

\mathcal{P}^{\varepsilon}:=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{P}}\delta_{\lfloor x+\varepsilon\rfloor},\qquad\mathcal{P}^{(n)}:=\sum_{i\in[n]}\delta_{\mathcal{Z}_{n}(i)-\lfloor\log_{\theta}n\rfloor},\quad\text{ and }\quad\varepsilon_{n}:=\log_{\theta}n-\lfloor\log_{\theta}n\rfloor,

then
\mathcal{P}^{\varepsilon}
and
\mathcal{P}^{(n)}
are random elements in
\mathcal{M}^{\#}_{\mathbb{Z}^{*}}
and we can provide conditions such that
\mathcal{P}^{(n_{\ell})}
converges weakly to
\mathcal{P}^{\varepsilon}
, for subsequences
(n_{\ell})_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
\varepsilon_{n_{\ell}}\to\varepsilon
as
\ell\to\infty
. We abuse notation to write
\mathcal{P}^{\varepsilon}(i)=\mathcal{P}^{\varepsilon}(\{i\})=|\{x\in\mathcal{P}:\lfloor x+\varepsilon\rfloor=i\}|=|\{x\in\mathcal{P}:x\in[i-\varepsilon,i+1-\varepsilon)\}|
.
The choice of the metric on
\mathbb{Z}^{*}
is convenient since, by Theorem 11.1.VII of
[
5
]
, weak convergence in
\mathcal{M}^{\#}_{\mathbb{Z}^{*}}
is equivalent to convergence of finite dimensional distributions, see Definition 11.1.IV of
[
5
]
. In particular, the weak convergence of
\mathcal{P}^{(n_{\ell})}
implies the convergence in distribution of
X_{\geq i}^{(n_{l})}=\mathcal{P}^{(n_{\ell})}[i,\infty)
.

We now state our main results, which we split into several theorems based on the cases in Assumption
2.3
.

Theorem 2.5

(High degrees in WRTs

(

Atom

)

case)
.

Consider the WRT model in Definition
2.1
with vertex-weights
(W_{i})_{i\in[n]}
that satisfy the

(

Atom

)

case in Assumption
2.3
for some
q_{0}\in(0,1]
. Fix
\varepsilon\in[0,1]
. Let
(n_{\ell})_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
be a positive integer sequence such that
\varepsilon_{n_{\ell}}\to\varepsilon
as
\ell\to\infty
. Then
\mathcal{P}^{(n_{\ell})}
converges weakly in
\mathcal{M}^{\#}_{\mathbb{Z}^{*}}
to
\mathcal{P}^{\varepsilon}
as
\ell\to\infty
. Equivalently, for any
i<i^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}
, jointly as
\ell\to\infty
,

(2.10)

(X^{(n_{\ell})}_{i},X^{(n_{\ell})}_{i+1},\ldots,X^{(n_{\ell})}_{i^{\prime}-1},X^{(n_{\ell})}_{\geq i^{\prime}})\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits^{d}}(\mathcal{P}^{\varepsilon}(i),\mathcal{P}^{\varepsilon}(i+1),\ldots,\mathcal{P}^{\varepsilon}(i^{\prime}-1),\mathcal{P}^{\varepsilon}([i^{\prime},\infty)).

We note that this result recovers and extends
[
1
, Theorem
1.2
]
, in which an equivalent result is presented which only holds for the random recursive tree, i.e. the particular case of the weighted recursive tree in which
q_{0}:=\mathbb{P}\!\left(W=1\right)=1
. In Theorem
2.5
we allow
q_{0}\in(0,1)
as well, under the additional condition

(

C

2
)

.

When the vertex-weight distribution belongs to the Weibull MDA, we can prove convergence in probability under a deterministic second-order scaling, but are unable to obtain what we conjecture to be a random third-order term similar to the result in Theorem
2.5
:

Theorem 2.6

(High degrees in WRTs,

(

Weibull

)

case)
.

Consider the WRT model in Definition
2.1
with vertex-weights
(W_{i})_{i\in[n]}
that satisfy the

(

Weibull

)

case in Assumption
2.3
for some
\alpha>1
. Then,

(2.11)

\max_{i\in[n]}\frac{\mathcal{Z}_{n}(i)-\log_{\theta}n}{\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits^{\mathbb{P}}}-(\alpha-1).

Finally, when the vertex-weight distribution belongs to the Gumbel MDA, we have similar results compared to the Weibull MDA case in the above theorem. Here we are also able to obtain a deterministic second-order scaling for both the
()
‣

(

Gumbel

)

and
()
‣

(

Gumbel

)

sub-cases, as well as a third- and fourth-order scaling for the
()
‣

(

Gumbel

)

sub-case:

Theorem 2.7

(High degrees in WRTs,

(

Gumbel

)

case)
.

Consider the WRT model in Definition
2.1
with vertex-weights
(W_{i})_{i\in[n]}
that satisfy the

(

Gumbel

)

case in Assumption
2.3
.

In the
()
‣

(

Gumbel

)

sub-case, let
\gamma:=1/(1+\tau)
. Then,

(2.12)

\max_{i\in[n]}\frac{\mathcal{Z}_{n}(i)-\log_{\theta}n}{(\log_{\theta}n)^{1-\gamma}}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits^{\mathbb{P}}}-\frac{\tau^{\gamma}}{(1-\gamma)\log\theta}\Big(\frac{1-\theta^{-1}}{c_{1}}\Big)^{1-\gamma}=:-C_{\theta,\tau,c_{1}}.

In the
()
‣

(

Gumbel

)

sub-case,

(2.13)

\max_{i\in[n]}\frac{\mathcal{Z}_{n}(i)-\log_{\theta}n+C_{1}(\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n)^{\tau}-C_{2}(\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n)^{\tau-1}\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n}{(\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n)^{\tau-1}}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits^{\mathbb{P}}}C_{3},

where

(2.14)

\displaystyle C_{1}

\displaystyle:=(\log\theta)^{\tau-1}c_{1}^{-\tau},\qquad C_{2}:=(\log\theta)^{\tau-1}\tau(\tau-1)c_{1}^{-\tau},

\displaystyle C_{3}

\displaystyle:=\big(\log_{\theta}(\log\theta)(\tau-1)\log\theta-\log(\mathrm{e}c_{1}^{\tau}(1-\theta^{-1})/\tau)\big)(\log\theta)^{\tau-2}\tau c_{1}^{-\tau}.

We see that only in the

(

Atom

)

case we are able to obtain the higher-order asymptotics up to random order. This is due to the fact that, in this particular case, the vertices with high degree all have vertex-weight one. In the other classes covered in Theorems
2.6
and
2.7
vertices with high degrees have a vertex-weight close to one, which causes their degrees to grow slightly slower. This results in the higher-order asymptotics as observed in these theorems.

We are able to obtain more precise results related to the maximum and near-maximum degree vertices in the

(

Atom

)

case as well, which again recover and extend the results in
[
1
]
.

Theorem 2.8

(Asymptotic tail distribution for maximum degree in

(

Atom

)

case)
.

Consider the WRT model in Definition
2.1
with vertex-weights
(W_{i})_{i\in[n]}
that satisfy the

(

Atom

)

case in Assumption
2.3
for some
q_{0}\in(0,1]
and recall
\varepsilon_{n}
from (
2.9
). Then, for any integer-valued
i_{n}=i(n)
with
i_{n}+\log_{\theta}n<(\theta/(\theta-1))\log n
and
\liminf_{n\to\infty}i_{n}>-\infty
,

(2.15)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(\max_{j\in[n]}\mathcal{Z}_{n}(j)\geq\lfloor\log_{\theta}n\rfloor+i_{n}\right)=\big(1-\exp(-q_{0}\theta^{-i_{n}+\varepsilon_{n}})\big)(1+o(1)).

Moreover, let
\mathcal{M}_{n}\subseteq[n]
denote the
(
random
)
set of vertices that attain the maximum degree in
T_{n}
, fix
\varepsilon\in[0,1]
, and let
(n_{\ell})_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
be a positive integer sequence such that
\varepsilon_{n_{\ell}}\to\varepsilon
as
\ell\to\infty
. Then,
|\mathcal{M}_{n_{\ell}}|\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits^{d}}M_{\varepsilon}
, where
M_{\varepsilon}
has distribution

(2.16)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(M_{\varepsilon}=k\right)=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{k!}\big(q_{0}(1-\theta^{-1})\theta^{-j+\varepsilon}\big)^{k}\mathrm{e}^{-q_{0}\theta^{-j+\varepsilon}},\qquad k\in\mathbb{N}.

Finally, we establish an asymptotic normality result for the number of vertices which have ‘near-maximum’ degrees. For a precise definition of ‘near-maximum’, we define sequences
(s_{k},r_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
as

(2.17)

\displaystyle s_{k}

\displaystyle:=\inf\big\{x\in(0,1):\mathbb{P}\!\left(W\in(x,1)\right)\leq\exp(-(1-\theta^{-1})(1-x)k)\big\},

\displaystyle r_{k}

\displaystyle:=\exp(-(1-\theta^{-1})(1-s_{k})k).

As a result,
r_{k}
can be used as the error term in the asymptotic expression of
p_{\geq k}
(as in (
2.4
)) when the weight distribution satisfies the

(

Atom

)

case (see Theorem
5.3
) and is essential in quantifying how much smaller ‘near-maximum’ degrees are relative to the maximum degree of the graph in this case. We note that
r_{k}
is decreasing and converges to zero with
k
(see Lemma
A.5
), and that in the definition of
s_{k}
and
r_{k}
we can allow the index to be continuous rather than just an integer (the proof of Lemma
A.5
can be adapted to still hold in this case). We can then formulate the following theorem:

Theorem 2.9

(Asymptotic normality of near-maximum degree vertices,

(

Atom

)

case)
.

Consider the WRT model in Definition
2.1
with vertex-weights
(W_{i})_{i\in[n]}
that satisfy the

(

Atom

)

case in Assumption
2.3
for some
q_{0}\in(0,1]
. Then, for an integer-valued
i_{n}=i(n)\to-\infty
such that
i_{n}=o(\log n\wedge|\log r_{\log_{\theta}n}|)
,

(2.18)

\frac{X^{(n)}_{i_{n}}-q_{0}(1-\theta^{-1})\theta^{-i_{n}+\varepsilon_{n}}}{\sqrt{q_{0}(1-\theta^{-1})\theta^{-i_{n}+\varepsilon_{n}}}}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits^{d}}N(0,1).

Remark 2.10
.

The constraint
i_{n}=o(\log n\wedge\log r_{\log_{\theta}n})
can be simplified by providing more information on the tail of the weight distribution. Only when
W
has an atom at one and support bounded away from one do we have that
o(\log n\wedge\log r_{\log_{\theta}n})=o(\log n)
. That is, when there exists an
s\in(0,1)
such that
\mathbb{P}\!\left(W\in(s,1)\right)=0
. In that case, we can set
s_{k}=s
and
r_{k}=\exp(-(1-\theta^{-1})(1-s)k)
for all
k
large, so that

(2.19)

\log r_{\log_{\theta}n}=-(1-\theta^{-1})(1-s)\log_{\theta}n,

so that indeed
o(\log n\wedge\log r_{\log_{\theta}n})=o(\log n)
. In all other cases it follows that
s_{k}\uparrow 1
, so that
\log r_{\log_{\theta}n}=o(\log n)
and the constraint simplifies to
i_{n}=o(\log r_{\log_{\theta}n})
.

Outline of the paper

In Section
3
we provide a short overview and intuitive idea of the proofs of Theorems
2.5
,
2.6
,
2.7
,
2.8
and
2.9
. In Section
4
we discuss two examples of vertex-weight distributions which satisfy the

(

Weibull

)

and

(

Gumbel

)

cases, respectively, for which more precise results can be obtained. We then provide the key concepts and results that are used in the proofs of the main theorems in Section
5
. We use these results to prove the main theorems in Section
6
. Finally, in Section
7
we provide the necessary techniques and results, comparable to what is presented in Section
5
, to prove the statements regarding the examples of Section
4
.

3.
Intuitive idea of (the proof of) the main theorems

We provide a short intuitive idea as to why the results stated in Section
2
hold.

The main elements in obtaining a more precise understanding of the behaviour of the maximum degree of the WRT are the following:

(i)

A precise expression of the tail distribution of the in-degree of uniformly at random selected vertices
(v_{\ell})_{\ell\in[k]}
, for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
. That is,

(3.1)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(\mathcal{Z}_{n}(v_{\ell})\geq m_{\ell}\text{ for all }\ell\in[k]\right)=\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}p_{\geq m_{\ell}}(1+o(n^{-\beta})),

for some
\beta>0
and where the
m_{\ell}\in\mathbb{N}
are such that
m_{\ell}\leq c\log n
for some
c\in(0,\theta/(\theta-1))
. This extends (
2.5
) in the sense of convergence in mean to
k\in\mathbb{N}
many uniformly at random selected vertices rather than just one, and allows the
m_{\ell}
to grow with
n
rather than being fixed. Moreover, the error term
1+o(n^{-\beta})
extends previously known results as well, for which no convergence rate was known.

(ii)

The asymptotic behaviour of
p_{\geq k}
, as defined in (
2.4
), as
k\to\infty
for each case in Assumption
2.3
.

Element

(
i
)

, which is proved in Proposition
5.1
, allows us to obtain bounds on the probability of the event
\{\max_{j\in[n]}\mathcal{Z}_{n}(j)\geq k_{n}\}
for any sequence
k_{n}\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
. These probabilities can be expressed in terms of
np_{\geq k_{n}}
(using union bounds and
the second moment method in the form of the Chung-Erdős inequality, as is shown in Lemma
5.8
). By

(

⁢
i
i

)

we can then precisely quantify
k_{n}
such that these bounds either tend to zero or one, which implies whether
\{\max_{j\in[n]}\mathcal{Z}_{n}(i)\geq k_{n}\}
does or does not hold with high probability. This is the main approach for Theorems
2.6
and
2.7
.

To obtain the random limits described in terms of the Poisson process
\mathcal{P}^{\varepsilon}
, as in Theorem
2.5
, we use a similar approach as in
[
1
]
. Both

(
i
)

and

(

⁢
i
i

)

are still essential, but are now used to obtain factorial moments of the quantities
X^{(n)}_{i}
and
X^{(n)}_{\geq i}
, defined in (
2.8
), as shown in Proposition
5.6
. More specifically, for any
i<i^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}
and
a_{i},\ldots,a_{i^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, and recalling that
(x)_{k}:=x(x-1)\ldots(x-(k-1))
,

(3.2)

\mathbb{E}\left[\Big(X^{(n)}_{\geq i^{\prime}}\Big)_{a_{i^{\prime}}}\prod_{k=i}^{i^{\prime}-1}\Big(X^{(n)}_{i}\Big)_{a_{i}}\right]=\Big(q_{0}\theta^{-i^{\prime}+\varepsilon_{n}}\Big)^{a_{i^{\prime}}}\prod_{k=i}^{i^{\prime}-1}\Big(q_{0}(1-\theta^{-1})\theta^{-k+\varepsilon_{n}}\Big)^{a_{k}}(1+o(1)).

We stress that the specific form of the right-hand side is due to the underlying assumption in Theorem
2.5
that the vertex-weight distribution has an atom at one, as in the

(

Atom

)

case of Assumption
2.3
. The error term can be specified in more detail, but we omit this as it serves no further purpose here. The result essentially follows directly from these estimates by observing that the right-hand side of (
3.2
) can be understood as an approximation of the factorial moment of the Poisson random variables
\mathcal{P}^{\varepsilon}([i-\varepsilon,i+1-\varepsilon)),\ldots,\mathcal{P}^{\varepsilon}([i^{\prime}-\varepsilon,\infty))
, when
\varepsilon_{n}
converges to some
\varepsilon
.

The equality in (
3.2
) follows from the fact that
X^{(n)}_{i}
and
X^{(n)}_{\geq i}
can be expressed as sums of indicator random variables of disjoint events, so that their factorial means can be understood via the probabilities in

(
i
)

. Then, again using the asymptotic behaviour of
p_{\geq k}
(as in

(

⁢
i
i

)

), allows us to obtain the right-hand side of (
3.2
).

Finally, Theorems
2.8
and
2.9
are also a result of (
3.2
). This is due to the fact that the events
\{\max_{j\in[n]}\mathcal{Z}_{n}(j)\geq\lfloor\log_{\theta}n\rfloor+i\}
can be understood via the events
\{X^{(n)}_{\geq i}>0\}
. Again using ideas similar to ones developed in
[
1
]
then allow us to obtain the results.

4.
Examples

In this section we discuss some particular choices of distributions for the vertex-weights for which more precise statements can be made compared to those stated in Section
2
. The reason we can improve on these more general results is due to a better understanding of the asymptotic behaviour of
p_{k}
and
p_{\geq k}
(see (
2.4
)) as
k\to\infty
. As discussed in Section
3
, to understand the asymptotic behaviour of the (near-)maximum degree(s) up to random order a very precise asymptotic expression for
p_{\geq k}
is required. Though not possible in general in the

(

Weibull

)

and

(

Gumbel

)

cases of Assumption
2.3
, certain choices of vertex-weight distributions do allow for a more explicit formulation of
p_{\geq k}
, yielding improved asymptotics. The proofs of the results presented here are deferred to Section
7
.

Example 4.1

(Beta distribution)
.

We consider a random variable
W
with a tail distribution

(4.1)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(W\geq x\right)=\int_{x}^{1}\frac{\Gamma(\alpha+\beta)}{\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(\beta)}s^{\alpha-1}(1-s)^{\beta-1}\,\mathrm{d}s,\qquad x\in[0,1),

for some
\alpha,\beta>0
. Clearly,
W
belongs to the

(

Weibull

)

case of Assumption
2.3
. Moreover,
W
satisfies condition

(

C

2
)

of Assumption
2.3
. Since for any
x_{0}\in(0,1)
we can bound
(1-s)^{\beta-1}\leq 1\vee(1-x_{0})^{\beta-1}
for all
s\in(0,x_{0})
, it follows that for any
x\in[0,x_{0}]
,

(4.2)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(W\leq x\right)=\int_{0}^{x}\frac{\Gamma(\alpha+\beta)}{\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(\beta)}s^{\alpha-1}(1-s)^{\beta-1}\,\mathrm{d}s\leq(1\vee(1-x_{0})^{\beta-1})\frac{\Gamma(\alpha+\beta)}{\Gamma(\alpha+1)\Gamma(\beta)}x^{\alpha},

so that condition

(

C

2
)

is satisfied with
C=(1\vee(1-x_{0})^{\beta-1})\Gamma(\alpha+\beta)/(\Gamma(\alpha+1)\Gamma(\beta))
, the constant
x_{0}\in(0,1)
arbitrary, and
\rho=\alpha
.

We set, for
\theta:=1+\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]\in(1,2),

(4.3)

\displaystyle X^{(n)}_{i}

\displaystyle:=|\{j\in[n]:\mathcal{Z}_{n}(j)=\lfloor\log_{\theta}n-\beta\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n\rfloor+i\}|,

\displaystyle X^{(n)}_{\geq i}

\displaystyle:=|\{j\in[n]:\mathcal{Z}_{n}(j)\geq\lfloor\log_{\theta}n-\beta\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n\rfloor+i\}|,

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{n}

\displaystyle:=(\log_{\theta}n-\beta\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n)-\lfloor\log_{\theta}n-\beta\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n\rfloor,

\displaystyle c_{\alpha,\beta,\theta}

\displaystyle:=\frac{\Gamma(\alpha+\beta)}{\Gamma(\alpha)}(1-\theta^{-1})^{-\beta}.

Then, we can formulate the following results.

Theorem 4.2
.

Consider the WRT model in Definition
2.1
with vertex-weights
(W_{i})_{i\in[n]}
whose distribution satisfies (
4.1
) for some
\alpha,\beta>0
, and recall
\varepsilon_{n}
and
c_{\alpha,\beta,\theta}
from (
4.3
). Fix
\varepsilon\in[0,1]
, let
(n_{\ell})_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
be an increasing integer sequence such that
\varepsilon_{n_{\ell}}\to\varepsilon
as
\ell\to\infty
and let
\mathcal{P}
be a Poisson point process on
\mathbb{R}
with intensity measure
\lambda(x)=c_{\alpha,\beta,\theta}\theta^{-x}\log\theta\,\mathrm{d}x
. Define

(4.4)

\mathcal{P}^{\varepsilon}:=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{P}}\delta_{\lfloor x+\varepsilon\rfloor}\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathcal{P}^{(n)}:=\sum_{i\in[n]}\delta_{\mathcal{Z}_{n}(i)-\lfloor\log_{\theta}n-\beta\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n\rfloor}.

Then in
\mathcal{M}^{\#}_{\mathbb{Z}^{*}}

(
the space of boundedly finite measures on
\mathbb{Z}^{*}=\mathbb{Z}\cup\{\infty\}
with the metric defined in Section
2
)
,
\mathcal{P}^{(n_{\ell})}
converges weakly to
\mathcal{P}^{\varepsilon}
as
\ell\to\infty
. Equivalently, for any
i<i^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}
, jointly as
\ell\to\infty
,

(4.5)

(X^{(n_{\ell})}_{i},X^{(n_{\ell})}_{i+1},\ldots,X^{(n_{\ell})}_{i^{\prime}-1},X^{(n_{\ell})}_{\geq i^{\prime}})\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits^{d}}(\mathcal{P}^{\varepsilon}(i),\mathcal{P}^{\varepsilon}(i+1),\ldots,\mathcal{P}^{\varepsilon}(i^{\prime}-1),\mathcal{P}^{\varepsilon}([i^{\prime},\infty))).

We remark that the second-order term
\beta\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n
is established in Theorem
2.6
as well and that the above theorem recovers this result and extends it to the random third-order term, which is similar to the result in Theorem
2.5
.

Theorem 4.3
.

Consider the WRT model in Definition
2.1
with vertex-weights
(W_{i})_{i\in[n]}
whose distribution satisfies (
4.1
) for some
\alpha,\beta>0
, and recall
\varepsilon_{n}
and
c_{\alpha,\beta,\theta}
from (
4.3
). Then, for any integer-valued
i_{n}
with
i_{n}=\delta\log_{\theta}n+o(\log n)
for some
\delta\in[0,\theta\log\theta/(\theta-1)-1)
and
\liminf_{n\to\infty}i_{n}>-\infty
,

(4.6)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}{}

\displaystyle\Big(\max_{j\in[n]}\mathcal{Z}_{n}(j)\geq\lfloor\log_{\theta}n-\beta\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n\rfloor+i_{n}\Big)=\big(1-\exp\big(-c_{\alpha,\beta,\theta}(1+\delta)^{-\beta}\theta^{-i_{n}+\varepsilon_{n}}\big)\big)(1+o(1)).

Moreover, let
\mathcal{M}_{n}\subseteq[n]
denote the (random) set of vertices that attain the maximum degree in
T_{n}
, fix
\varepsilon\in[0,1]
and let
(n_{\ell})_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
be a positive integer sequence such that
\varepsilon_{n_{\ell}}\to\varepsilon
as
\ell\to\infty
. Then,
|\mathcal{M}_{n_{\ell}}|\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits^{d}}M_{\varepsilon}
, where
M_{\varepsilon}
has distribution

(4.7)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(M_{\varepsilon}=k\right)=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{k!}\big(c_{\alpha,\beta,\theta}(1-\theta^{-1})\theta^{-j+\varepsilon}\Big)^{k}\exp\big(-c_{\alpha,\beta,\theta}\theta^{-j+\varepsilon}\big),\qquad k\in\mathbb{N}.

Theorem 4.4
.

Consider the WRT model in Definition
2.1
with vertex-weights
(W_{i})_{i\in[n]}
whose distribution satisfies (
4.1
) for some
\alpha,\beta>0
and recall
\varepsilon_{n}
and
c_{\alpha,\beta,\theta}
from (
4.3
). Then, for an integer-valued
i_{n}\to-\infty
such that
i_{n}=o(\log\log n)
,

(4.8)

\frac{X^{(n)}_{i_{n}}-c_{\alpha,\beta,\theta}(1-\theta^{-1})\theta^{-i_{n}+\varepsilon_{n}}}{\sqrt{c_{\alpha,\beta,\theta}(1-\theta^{-1})\theta^{-i_{n}+\varepsilon_{n}}}}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits^{d}}N(0,1).

The three theorems are the analogue of Theorems
2.5
,
2.8
and
2.9
, respectively, where we now consider vertex-weights distributed according to a beta distribution rather than a distribution with an atom at one.

Example 4.5

(Fraction of ‘gamma’ random variables)
.

We consider a random variable
W
with a tail distribution

(4.9)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(W\geq x\right)=(1-x)^{-b}\mathrm{e}^{-x/(c_{1}(1-x))},\qquad x\in[0,1),

for some
b\in\mathbb{R},c_{1}>0
with
bc_{1}\leq 1
. The random variable
(1-W)^{-1}
belongs to the Gumbel maximum domain of attraction, as

(4.10)

\mathbb{P}\!\left((1-W)^{-1}\geq x\right)=\mathbb{P}\!\left(W\geq 1-1/x\right)=\mathrm{e}^{1/c_{1}}x^{b}\mathrm{e}^{-x/c_{1}},\qquad x\geq 1,

so that
W
belongs to the Gumbel MDA as well by
[
15
, Lemma
2.6
]
, and satisfies the

(

Gumbel

)

-
()
‣

(

Gumbel

)

sub-case with
a=\mathrm{e}^{1/c_{1}},b\in\mathbb{R},c_{1}>0
, and
\tau=1
. As a result,
X:=(1-W)^{-1}
is a ‘gamma’ random variable in the sense that its tail distribution is asymptotically equal to that of a gamma random variable, up to constants. We can then write
W=(X-1)/X
, a fraction of these ‘gamma’ random variables.

Let
f_{W}
denote the probability density function of
W
. It follows from (
4.9
) that

(4.11)

f_{W}(x)=(1/c_{1}–b(1-x))(1-x)^{-(b+2)}\mathrm{e}^{-x/(c_{1}(1-x))}.

Observe that the requirement
bc_{1}\leq 1
ensures that
f_{W}(x)\geq 0
for all
x\in[0,1)
. As
\lim_{x\uparrow 1}f_{W}(x)=0
and the density is bounded from above in a neighbourhood of the origin, it follows that
f_{W}(x)\leq C
for some
C>0
and all
x\in[0,1)
. As a result, this distribution satisfies condition

(

C

2
)

of Assumption
2.3
with
\rho=1
,
x_{0}\in(0,1)
arbitrary, and some sufficiently large
C>0
.

Recall
C_{\theta,\tau,c_{1}}
from (
2.12
). We set, for
\theta:=1+\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]\in(1,2),

(4.12)

C:=\mathrm{e}^{c_{1}^{-1}(1-\theta^{-1})/2}\sqrt{\pi}c_{1}^{-1/4+b/2}(1-\theta^{-1})^{1/4+b/2},\qquad\text{and}\quad c_{c_{1},b,\theta}:=C\theta^{C_{\theta,1,c_{1}}^{2}/2},

and

(4.13)

\displaystyle X^{(n)}_{i}:={}

\displaystyle\big|\big\{j\in[n]:\mathcal{Z}_{n}(j)=\big\lfloor\log_{\theta}n-C_{\theta,1,c_{1}}\sqrt{\log_{\theta}n}+(b/2+1/4)\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n\big\rfloor+i\big\}\big|,

\displaystyle X^{(n)}_{\geq i}:={}

\displaystyle\big|\big\{j\in[n]:\mathcal{Z}_{n}(j)\geq\big\lfloor\log_{\theta}n-C_{\theta,1,c_{1}}\sqrt{\log_{\theta}n}+(b/2+1/4)\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n\big\rfloor+i\big\}\big|,

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{n}:={}

\displaystyle\big(\log_{\theta}n-C_{\theta,1,c_{1}}\sqrt{\log_{\theta}n}+(b/2+1/4)\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n\big)

\displaystyle-\big\lfloor\log_{\theta}n-C_{\theta,1,c_{1}}\sqrt{\log_{\theta}n}+(b/2+1/4)\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n\big\rfloor.

Then, we can formulate the following results.

Theorem 4.6
.

Consider the WRT model in Definition
2.1
with vertex-weights
(W_{i})_{i\in[n]}
whose distribution satisfies (
4.9
) for some
b\in\mathbb{R},c_{1}>0
such that
bc_{1}\leq 1
, and recall
C_{\theta,\tau,c_{1}}
from (
2.13
). Then,

(4.14)

\max_{i\in[n]}\frac{\mathcal{Z}_{n}(i)-\log_{\theta}n+C_{\theta,1,c_{1}}\sqrt{\log_{\theta}n}}{\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits^{\mathbb{P}}}\frac{b}{2}+\frac{1}{4}.

Furthermore, recall
\varepsilon_{n}
from (
4.13
), fix
\varepsilon\in[0,1]
, and let
(n_{\ell})_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
be an increasing integer sequence such that
\varepsilon_{n_{\ell}}\to\varepsilon
as
\ell\to\infty
. Let
\mathcal{P}
be a Poisson point process on
\mathbb{R}
with intensity measure
\lambda(x)=c_{c_{1},b,\theta}\theta^{-x}\log\theta\,\mathrm{d}x
, where we recall
c_{c_{1},b,\theta}
from (
4.12
). Define

(4.15)

\mathcal{P}^{\varepsilon}:=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{P}}\delta_{\lfloor x+\varepsilon\rfloor}\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathcal{P}^{(n)}:=\sum_{i\in[n]}\delta_{\mathcal{Z}_{n}(i)-\lfloor\log_{\theta}n-C_{\theta,1,c_{1}}\sqrt{\log_{\theta}n}+(b/2+1/4)\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n\rfloor}.

Then in
\mathcal{M}^{\#}_{\mathbb{Z}^{*}}

(
the space of boundedly finite measures on
\mathbb{Z}^{*}=\mathbb{Z}\cup\{\infty\}
with the metric defined in Section
2
)
,
\mathcal{P}^{(n_{\ell})}
converges weakly to
\mathcal{P}^{\varepsilon}
as
\ell\to\infty
. Equivalently, for any
i<i^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}
, jointly as
\ell\to\infty
,

(4.16)

(X^{(n_{\ell})}_{i},X^{(n_{\ell})}_{i+1},\ldots,X^{(n_{\ell})}_{i^{\prime}-1},X^{(n_{\ell})}_{\geq i^{\prime}})\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits^{d}}(\mathcal{P}^{\varepsilon}(i),\mathcal{P}^{\varepsilon}(i+1),\ldots,\mathcal{P}^{\varepsilon}(i^{\prime}-1),\mathcal{P}^{\varepsilon}([i^{\prime},\infty))).

We remark that the second-order term in (
4.14
) is established in Theorem
2.7
, (
2.12
), as well. The above theorem recovers this former result and extends it to the third-order rescaling and to the random fourth-order term, which is similar to the result in Theorem
2.5
.

Theorem 4.7
.

Consider the WRT model in Definition
2.1
with vertex-weights
(W_{i})_{i\in[n]}
whose distribution satisfies (
4.9
) for some
b\in\mathbb{R},c_{1}>0
such that
bc_{1}\leq 1
, and recall
C_{\theta,\tau,c_{1}}
,
c_{c_{1},b,\theta}
, and
\varepsilon_{n}
from (
2.13
), (
4.12
), and (
4.13
), respectively. Then, for any integer-valued
i_{n}
with
i_{n}=\delta\sqrt{\log_{\theta}n}+o(\sqrt{\log n})
for some
\delta\geq 0
and
\liminf_{n\to\infty}i_{n}>-\infty
,

(4.17)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}{}

\displaystyle\Big(\max_{j\in[n]}\mathcal{Z}_{n}(j)\geq\lfloor\log_{\theta}n-C_{\theta,1,c_{1}}\sqrt{\log_{\theta}n}+(b/2+1/4)\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n\rfloor+i_{n}\Big)

\displaystyle=\big(1-\exp\big(-c_{c_{1},b,\theta}\theta^{-i_{n}+\varepsilon_{n}-\delta C_{\theta,1,c_{1}}/2}\big)\big)(1+o(1)).

Moreover, let
\mathcal{M}_{n}\subseteq[n]
denote the
(
random
)
set of vertices that attain the maximum degree in
T_{n}
, fix
\varepsilon\in[0,1]
and let
(n_{\ell})_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
be a positive integer sequence such that
\varepsilon_{n_{\ell}}\to\varepsilon
as
\ell\to\infty
. Then,
|\mathcal{M}_{n_{\ell}}|\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits^{d}}M_{\varepsilon}
, where
M_{\varepsilon}
has distribution

(4.18)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(M_{\varepsilon}=k\right)=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{k!}\big(c_{c_{1},b,\theta}(1-\theta^{-1})\theta^{-j+\varepsilon}\big)^{k}\exp\big(-c_{c_{1},b,\theta}\theta^{-j+\varepsilon}\big),\qquad k\in\mathbb{N}.

Theorem 4.8
.

Consider the WRT model in Definition
2.1
with vertex-weights
(W_{i})_{i\in[n]}
whose distribution satisfies (
4.9
) for some
b\in\mathbb{R},c_{1}>0
such that
bc_{1}\leq 1
, and recall
c_{c_{1},b,\theta}
and
\varepsilon_{n}
from (
4.12
) and (
4.13
), respectively. Then, for an integer-valued
i_{n}\to-\infty
such that
i_{n}=o(\log\log n)
,

(4.19)

\frac{X^{(n)}_{i_{n}}-c_{c_{1},b,\theta}(1-\theta^{-1})\theta^{-i_{n}+\varepsilon_{n}}}{\sqrt{c_{c_{1},b,\theta}(1-\theta^{-1})\theta^{-i_{n}+\varepsilon_{n}}}}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits^{d}}N(0,1).

The three theorems are the analogue of Theorems
2.5
,
2.8
and
2.9
, respectively, where we now consider vertex-weights distributed according to a distribution as in (
4.9
) rather than a distribution with an atom at one.

In both examples we see that a better understanding of the asymptotic behaviour of the tail of the degree distribution,
(p_{\geq k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
, allows us to identify the higher-order asymptotic behaviour of the (near-)maximum degree(s). It also shows that a higher-order random limit as in the sense of Theorems
4.2
and
4.6
is not expressed just by vertex-weights whose distribution has an atom at one, and we conjecture that this result is in fact universal for
all
vertex-weights distributions with bounded support.

5.
Degree tail distributions and factorial moments

In this section we state and prove the key elements required to prove the main results as stated in Section
2
. We stress that the results presented and proved in this section cover all the classes introduced in Assumption
2.3
(in fact, they cover any vertex-weight
W
that satisfies conditions

(

C

1
)

and

(

C

2
)

) and that the distinction between the classes of Assumption
2.3
follows in Section
6
. The intermediate results presented here form the main technical contribution, and their proofs are the main body of the paper.

5.1.
Statement of intermediate results and main ideas

As discussed in Section
3
, to understand the asymptotic behaviour of the maximum degree and near-maximum degrees we require a more precise understanding of the convergence in mean of the empirical degree distribution. To that end, we present the following result:

Proposition 5.1

(Distribution of typical vertex degrees)
.

Let
W
be a positive random variable that satisfies conditions

(

C

1
)

and

(

C

2
)

of Assumption
2.3
. Consider the WRT model in Definition
2.1
with vertex-weights
(W_{i})_{i\in[n]}
which are i.i.d. copies of
W
, fix
k\in\mathbb{N}
, and let
(v_{\ell})_{\ell\in[k]}
be
k
vertices selected uniformly at random without replacement from
[n]
. For a fixed
c\in(0,\theta/(\theta-1))
, there exist
\beta\geq\beta^{\prime}>0
such that uniformly over non-negative integers
m_{\ell}<c\log n
with
\ell\in[k]
,

(5.1)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\!\left(\mathcal{Z}_{n}(v_{\ell})=m_{\ell},\text{ for all }\ell\in[k]\right)

\displaystyle=\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]+W}\Big(\frac{W}{\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]+W}\Big)^{m_{\ell}}\right]\big(1+o\big(n^{-\beta}\big)\big),

and

(5.2)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\!\left(\mathcal{Z}_{n}(v_{\ell})\geq m_{\ell},\text{ for all }\ell\in[k]\right)

\displaystyle=\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\mathbb{E}\left[\Big(\frac{W}{\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]+W}\Big)^{m_{\ell}}\right]\big(1+o\big(n^{-\beta^{\prime}}\big)\big).

Remark 5.2
.

(i)
In
[
6
, Lemma
1
]
, it is proved that the degrees
(\mathcal{Z}_{n}(j))_{j\in[n]}
are negative quadrant dependent when considering the random recursive tree model (the WRT with deterministic weights, all equal to
1
). That is, for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
and any
j_{1}\neq\ldots\neq j_{k}\in[n]
and
m_{1},\ldots,m_{k}\in\mathbb{N}
,

(5.3)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(\bigcap_{\ell=1}^{k}\{\mathcal{Z}_{n}(j_{\ell})\geq m_{\ell}\}\right)\leq\prod_{j=1}^{k}\mathbb{P}\!\left(\mathcal{Z}_{n}(j_{\ell})\geq m_{\ell}\right).

This property only holds for the conditional probability measure
\mathbb{P}_{W}
when considering the WRT (or, more generally, the WRG) model, as follows from
[
15
, Lemma
7.1
]
, and can be obtained ‘asymptotically’ for the probability measure
\mathbb{P}
, as in the proof of
[
15
, Theorem
2.8
, Bounded case]
. Proposition
5.1
improves on this by establishing
asymptotic independence
under the non-conditional probability measure
\mathbb{P}
of the degrees of typical vertices, which allows us to extend the results in
[
15
]
to more precise asymptotics.

(ii)
We note that the result only requires the two main conditions in Assumption
2.3
on the behaviour of the vertex-weight distribution close to zero and one. Hence, results for other vertex-weight distributions that do not satisfy any of the particular cases outlined in this assumption can be obtained as well using the methods presented in this paper.

(iii)
The result in Proposition
5.1
improves on known results, especially those in
[
9
,
12
]
. In these papers similar techniques are used to prove a weaker result, in which the
m_{\ell}
are not allowed to diverge with
n
and where no convergence rate is provided.

(iv)
The constraint
c\in(0,\theta/(\theta-1))
arises from the following: when
m=c\log n+o(\log n)
, as we shall encounter later in the proof of Lemma
5.11
, see Equation (
5.144
), for
\varepsilon>0
small,

(5.4)

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i<n^{\varepsilon}}\mathbb{P}\!\left(\mathcal{Z}_{n}(v_{1})\geq m\right)\leq\exp\Big(\log n\Big(-(1-\varepsilon)+(c+o(1))\Big(1-\frac{1}{c(\theta-1)}+\log\Big(\frac{1}{c(\theta-1)}\Big)\Big)\Big)\Big).

We want the contribution of left-hand side, compared to the left-hand side of (
5.2
) to be negligible. Hence, it needs to be
o(p_{\geq m})=o(\theta^{-m})
(recall
p_{\geq m}
from (
2.4
)). We thus have the requirement that

(5.5)

-(1-\varepsilon)+c\Big(1-\frac{1}{c(\theta-1)}+\log\Big(\frac{1}{c(\theta-1)}\Big)\Big)>-c\log\theta

is satisfied. The inequality in (
5.5
) becomes an equality exactly when
c=\theta/(\theta-1)
and
\varepsilon=0
. Hence the need for
c
to be strictly smaller than
\theta/(\theta-1)
. Also, a more technical reason is that certain error terms no longer converge to zero with
n
when
c\geq\theta/(\theta-1)
. Moreover, we observe that
\frac{1}{\log\theta}<\frac{\theta}{\theta-1}
when
\theta\in(1,2]
, so that Proposition
5.1
covers degrees that extend
beyond
the value of the maximum degree (see (
2.6
)). This is a crucial element of Proposition
5.1
and the reason that strong claims regarding the largest degrees in the tree can be proved.

To use this (tail) distribution of
k
typical vertices
v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}
, a precise expression for the expected values on the right-hand side in Proposition
5.1
is required. Recall
p_{k}
from (
2.4
). The following theorem comes from
[
15
, Theorem
2.7
]
, in which the maximum degree of weighted recursive graphs is studied for a large class of vertex-weight distribution and in which asymptotic expressions of
p_{k}
are presented.

Theorem 5.3

(
[
15
]
, Asymptotic behaviour of
p_{k}
)
.

Recall that
\theta:=1+\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]
. We consider the different cases with respect to the vertex-weights as in Assumption
2.3
, but do not require condition

(

C

2
)

to hold.

(\mathrm{\mathbf{Atom}})

Recall that
q_{0}=\mathbb{P}\!\left(W=1\right)>0
and recall
r_{k}
from (
2.17
). Then,

(5.6)

\hskip 56.9055ptp_{k}=q_{0}(1-\theta^{-1})\theta^{-k}\big(1+\mathcal{O}(r_{k})\big).

(\mathrm{\mathbf{Weibull}})

Recall that
\alpha>1
is the power-law exponent. Then, for all
k>1/\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]
,

(5.7)

\underline{L}(k)k^{-(\alpha-1)}\theta^{-k}\leq p_{k}\leq\overline{L}(k)k^{-(\alpha-1)}\theta^{-k},

where
\underline{L}
and
\overline{L}
are slowly varying at infinity.

(\mathrm{\mathbf{Gumbel}})

(i)

If
W
satisfies the
()
‣

(

Gumbel

)

sub-case with parameter
\tau>0
, set
\gamma:=1/(\tau+1)
. Then,

(5.8)

\displaystyle p_{k}=\exp\Big(-\frac{\tau^{\gamma}}{1-\gamma}\Big(\frac{(1-\theta^{-1})k}{c_{1}}\Big)^{1-\gamma}(1+o(1))\Big)\theta^{-k}.

(ii)

If
W
satisfies the
()
‣

(

Gumbel

)

sub-case with parameter
\tau>1
,

(5.9)

\displaystyle\hskip 51.21504ptp_{k}=\exp\Big(-\Big(\frac{\log k}{c_{1}}\Big)^{\tau}\Big(1-\tau(\tau-1)\frac{\log\log k}{\log k}+\frac{K_{\tau,c_{1},\theta}}{\log k}(1+o(1))\Big)\Big)\theta^{-k}.

where
K_{\tau,c_{1},\theta}:=\tau\log(\mathrm{e}c_{1}^{\tau}(1-\theta^{-1})/\tau)
.

Remark 5.4
.

Equivalent upper and lower bounds can be obtained for
p_{\geq k}
as in (
2.4
) (with
m=1
), by adjusting the multiplicative constants in front of the asymptotic expressions by a factor
(1-\theta^{-1})^{-1}
only. This is due to the fact that

(5.10)

(1-\theta^{-1})^{-1}p_{k}=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{1-\theta^{-1}}\frac{\theta-1}{\theta-1+W}\Big(\frac{W}{\theta-1+W}\Big)^{k}\right]\geq\mathbb{E}\left[\Big(\frac{W}{\theta-1+W}\Big)^{k}\right]=p_{\geq k}\geq p_{k}.

Equivalently, the proof of
[
15
, Theorem
2.7
]
can be adapted to work for
p_{\geq k}
in which case the same asymptotic behaviour is established for
p_{\geq k}
, but with different constants in the front.

We also provide less precise but more general bounds on the degree distribution.

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
W
be a positive random variable with
x_{0}:=\sup\{x>0:\mathbb{P}\!\left(W\leq x\right)<1\}=1
. Then, for any
\xi>0
and
k
sufficiently large,

(5.11)

(\theta+\xi)^{-k}\leq p_{k}\leq p_{\geq k}\leq\theta^{-k}.

Proof.

The upper bound on
p_{\geq k}
directly follows from the fact that
x\mapsto(x/(\theta-1+x))^{k}
is increasing in
x
, so that

(5.12)

p_{\geq k}=\mathbb{E}\left[\Big(\frac{W}{\theta-1+W}\Big)^{k}\right]\leq\Big(\frac{1}{\theta-1+1}\Big)^{k}=\theta^{-k}.

For the lower bound, let us take some
\delta\in(0,\xi/(\theta-1+\xi))
and define

(5.13)

f_{k}(\theta,x):=\frac{\theta-1}{\theta-1+x}\Big(\frac{x}{\theta-1+x}\Big)^{k}.

Note that
p_{k}=\mathbb{E}\left[f_{k}(\theta,W)\right]
. Then, since
f_{k}(\theta,x)
is increasing in
x
on
(0,1]
for
k
sufficiently large,

(5.14)

\mathbb{E}\left[f_{k}(\theta,W)\right]\geq\mathbb{E}\left[f_{k}(\theta,W)\mathbbm{1}_{\{W>1-\delta\}}\right]\geq\mathbb{P}\!\left(W>1-\delta\right)\frac{\theta-1}{\theta-\delta}\Big(\frac{1-\delta}{\theta-\delta}\Big)^{k}.

We note that, since
x_{0}=1
, it holds that
\mathbb{P}\!\left(W>1-\delta\right)>0
for any
\delta>0
. Now, by the choice of
\delta
, we have
(\theta+\xi)(1-\delta)/(\theta-\delta)>1
, so we can find some
\zeta>0
sufficiently small so that

(5.15)

\mathbb{E}\left[f_{k}(\theta,W)\right](\theta+\xi)^{k}\geq\mathbb{P}\!\left(W>1-\delta\right)\frac{\theta-1}{\theta-\delta}\Big(\frac{(\theta+\xi)(1-\delta)}{\theta-\delta}\Big)^{k}\geq(1+\zeta)^{k}\geq 1,

as required. ∎

Recall the definition of
X_{i}^{(n)},X_{\geq i}^{(n)}
and
\varepsilon_{n}
from (
2.8
) and (
2.9
), respectively. Proposition
5.1
combined with Theorem
5.3
then allows us to obtain the following result.

Proposition 5.6

(Factorial moments when vertex-weights satisfy the

(

Atom

)

case)
.

Consider the WRT model as in Definition
2.1
with vertex-weights
(W_{i})_{i\in[n]}
that satisfy the

(

Atom

)

case in Assumption
2.3
for some
q_{0}\in(0,1]
. Recall
r_{k}
from (
2.17
), recall that
\theta:=1+\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]
, and that
(x)_{k}:=x(x-1)\cdots(x-(k-1))
for
x\in\mathbb{R},k\in\mathbb{N}
, and
(x)_{0}:=1
. For fixed
K\in\mathbb{N}
and
c\in(0,\theta/(\theta-1))
, there exists
\beta>0
such that the following holds. For any integer-valued
i_{n}
and
i_{n}^{\prime},
such that
0<i_{n}+\log_{\theta}n<i_{n}^{\prime}+\log_{\theta}n<c\log n
and any
(a_{j})_{i_{n}\leq j\leq i_{n}^{\prime}}
non-negative integer sequence such that
\sum_{j=i_{n}}^{i_{n}^{\prime}}a_{j}=K
,

(5.16)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\Big(X^{(n)}_{\geq i_{n}^{\prime}}\Big)_{a_{i_{n}^{\prime}}}\prod_{k=i_{n}}^{i_{n}^{\prime}-1}\Big(X_{k}^{(n)}\Big)_{a_{k}}\right]={}

\displaystyle\Big(q_{0}\theta^{-i_{n}^{\prime}+\varepsilon_{n}}\Big)^{a_{i_{n}^{\prime}}}\prod_{k=i_{n}}^{i_{n}^{\prime}-1}\Big(q_{0}(1-\theta^{-1})\theta^{-k+\varepsilon_{n}}\Big)^{a_{k}}

\displaystyle\times\big(1+\mathcal{O}\big(r_{\lfloor\log_{\theta}n\rfloor+i_{n}}\vee n^{-\beta}\big)\big).

Remark 5.7
.

(i)
Intuitively, the result of this proposition tells us that the random variables
(X^{(n)}_{k})_{k=i_{n}}^{i_{n}^{\prime}-1}
and
X(n)_{\geq i_{n}^{\prime}}
are, jointly, approximately Poisson distributed, since their factorial moments approximate those of Poisson random variables. This result hence plays an essential role in proving the weak convergence to a Poisson point process in Theorem
2.5
.

(ii)
Related to Remark
2.10
, the error term decays polynomially only if
W
has an atom at one and support bounded away from one and
\log_{\theta}n+i_{n}>\eta\log n
for some
\eta>0
. That is, when there exists an
s\in(0,1)
such that
\mathbb{P}\!\left(W\in(s,1)\right)=0
. In that case,
s_{k}\leq s
and
r_{k}\leq\exp(-(1-\theta^{-1})(1-s)k)
for all
k
large, so that

(5.17)

r_{\lfloor\log_{\theta}n\rfloor+i_{n}}\vee n^{-\beta}\leq\exp(-(1-\theta^{-1})(1-s)\eta\log n)\vee n^{-\beta}=n^{-\min\{\eta(1-\theta^{-1})(1-s),\beta\}}.

In all other cases, the error term decays slower than polynomially.

Proof of Proposition
5.6
subject to Proposition
5.1
.

We closely follow the approach in
[
1
, Proposition
2.1
]
, where an analogue result is presented for the case
q_{0}=1
, i.e. for the random recursive tree. Set
K^{\prime}:=K-a_{i_{n}^{\prime}}
and for each
i_{n}\leq k\leq i_{n}^{\prime}
and each
u\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\sum_{\ell=i_{n}}^{k-1}a_{\ell}<u\leq\sum_{\ell=i_{n}}^{k}a_{\ell}
, let
m_{u}=\lfloor\log_{\theta}n\rfloor+k
. We note that
m_{u}<\log_{\theta}n+i_{n}^{\prime}<c\log n
, so that the results in Proposition
5.1
can be used. Also, let
(v_{u})_{u\in[K]}
be
K
vertices selected uniformly at random without replacement from
[n]
, and define
I:=[K]\backslash[K^{\prime}]
. Then, as the
X^{(n)}_{\geq k}
and
X^{(n)}_{k}
can be expressed as sums of indicators,

(5.18)

\displaystyle\hskip-5.69046pt\mathbb{E}\bigg[\!\Big(X^{(n)}_{\geq i_{n}^{\prime}}\Big)_{a_{i_{n}^{\prime}}}\!\prod_{k=i_{n}}^{i_{n}^{\prime}-1}\!\Big(X_{k}^{(n)}\Big)_{a_{k}}\!\bigg]

\displaystyle=(n)_{K}\mathbb{P}\!\left(\mathcal{Z}_{n}(v_{u})=m_{u},\mathcal{Z}_{n}(v_{w})\geq m_{w}\text{ for all }u\in[K^{\prime}],w\in I\right)

\displaystyle=(n)_{K}\sum_{\ell=0}^{K^{\prime}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\subseteq[K^{\prime}]\\
|S|=\ell\end{subarray}}\!\!(-1)^{\ell}\mathbb{P}\!\left(\mathcal{Z}_{n}(v_{u})\geq m_{u}+\mathbbm{1}_{\{u\in S\}}\text{ for all }u\in[K]\right),

where the second step follows from
[
1
, Lemma
5.1
]
and is based on an inclusion-exclusion argument. We can now use Proposition
5.1
. First, we note that there exists a
\beta>0
such that for non-negative integers
m_{1},\ldots,m_{K}<c\log n
,

(5.19)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(\mathcal{Z}_{n}(v_{u})\geq m_{u}+\mathbbm{1}_{\{u\in S\}}\text{ for all }u\in[K]\right)=\prod_{u=1}^{K}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\Big(\frac{W}{\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]+W}\Big)^{m_{u}+\mathbbm{1}_{\{u\in S\}}}\bigg]\big(1+o\big(n^{-\beta}\big)\big).

Now, by Theorem
5.3
and the definition of
r_{k}
in (
2.17
) and as
r_{k}
is decreasing by Lemma
A.5
in the
Appendix
, when
|S|=\ell
,

(5.20)

\prod_{u=1}^{K}\mathbb{E}\left[\Big(\frac{W}{\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]+W}\Big)^{m_{u}+\mathbbm{1}_{\{u\in S\}}}\right]=q_{0}^{K}\theta^{-\ell-\sum_{u=1}^{K}m_{u}}\big(1+\mathcal{O}\big(r_{\lfloor\log_{\theta}n\rfloor+i_{n}}\vee n^{-\beta^{\prime}}\big)\big),

as the smallest
m_{u}
equals
\lfloor\log_{\theta}n\rfloor+i_{n}
. We have

(5.21)

\displaystyle(n)_{K}\sum_{\ell=0}^{K^{\prime}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\subseteq[K^{\prime}]\\
|S|=\ell\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\ell}q_{0}^{K}\theta^{-\ell-\sum_{u=1}^{K}m_{u}}

\displaystyle=(n)_{K}q_{0}^{K}\theta^{-K^{\prime}-\sum_{u=1}^{K}m_{u}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{K^{\prime}}\binom{K^{\prime}}{\ell}(-1)^{\ell}\theta^{K^{\prime}-\ell}

\displaystyle=(n)_{K}q_{0}^{K}(1-\theta^{-1})^{K^{\prime}}\theta^{-\sum_{u=1}^{K}m_{u}}.

We then observe that
(n)_{K}=\theta^{K\log_{\theta}n}(1+\mathcal{O}(1/n))
. Moreover, we recall that
K=\sum_{k=i_{n}}^{i_{n}^{\prime}}a_{k}
and
K^{\prime}=\sum_{k=i_{n}}^{i_{n}^{\prime}-1}a_{k}
, while
m_{u}=\lfloor\log_{\theta}n\rfloor+k
if
\sum_{\ell=i_{n}}^{k-1}a_{\ell}\leq u<\sum_{\ell=i_{n}}^{k}a_{\ell}
for
i_{n}\leq\ell\leq i_{n}^{\prime}
; in addition, recall
\varepsilon_{n}
from (
2.9
). Using (
5.21
) combined with (
5.19
) and (
5.20
) in (
5.18
), we obtain

(5.22)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg[\!\Big(\!X^{(n)}_{\geq i_{n}^{\prime}}\!\Big)_{a_{i_{n}^{\prime}}}\!\prod_{k=i_{n}}^{i_{n}^{\prime}-1}\!\Big(\!X_{k}^{(n)}\!\Big)_{a_{k}}\!\bigg]\!={}

\displaystyle q_{0}^{K}(1-\theta^{-1})^{K^{\prime}}\theta^{K\log_{\theta}n-\sum_{u=1}^{K}m_{u}}\big(1+\mathcal{O}\big(r_{\lfloor\log_{\theta}n\rfloor+i_{n}}\vee n^{-\beta}\big)\big)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\Big(q_{0}\theta^{-i_{n}^{\prime}+\varepsilon_{n}}\Big)^{a_{i_{n}^{\prime}}}\prod_{k=i_{n}}^{i_{n}^{\prime}-1}\Big(q_{0}(1-\theta^{-1})\theta^{-k+\varepsilon_{n}}\Big)^{a_{k}}

\displaystyle\times\big(1+\mathcal{O}\big(r_{\lfloor\log_{\theta}n\rfloor+i_{n}}\vee n^{-\beta}\big)\big),

as desired.
∎

The next lemma builds on
[
15
, Lemma
7.1
]
and
[
6
, Lemma
1
]
and provides bounds on the maximum degree that hold with high probability.

Lemma 5.8
.

Let
W
be a positive random variable that satisfies conditions

(

C

1
)

and

(

C

2
)

of Assumption
2.3
. Consider the WRT model in Definition
2.1
with vertex-weights
(W_{i})_{i\in[n]}
which are i.i.d. copies of
W
. Fix
c\in(0,\theta/(\theta-1))
and let
(k_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a non-negative, diverging integer sequence such that
k_{n}<c\log n
and let
v_{1}
be a vertex selected uniformly at random from
[n]
. If
\lim_{n\to\infty}n\mathbb{P}\!\left(\mathcal{Z}_{n}(v_{1})\geq k_{n}\right)=0
, then

(5.23)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\!\left(\max_{i\in[n]}\mathcal{Z}_{n}(i)\geq k_{n}\right)=0.

Similarly, when instead
\lim_{n\to\infty}n\mathbb{P}\!\left(\mathcal{Z}_{n}(v_{1})\geq k_{n}\right)=\infty
,

(5.24)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\!\left(\max_{i\in[n]}\mathcal{Z}_{n}(i)\geq k_{n}\right)=1.

Remark 5.9
.

Similar to what is discussed in Remark
5.2
(i)
, the result in this lemma is stronger than the results presented in
[
15
, Lemma
7.1
]
and
[
6
, Lemma
1
]
. It extends the latter to the WRT model rather than just the RRT model, and improves the former as the result holds for the non-conditional probability measure
\mathbb{P}
rather than
\mathbb{P}_{W}
, which is what is used in
[
15
]
. Due to the difficulties of working with the conditional probability measure, only a first order asymptotic result can be proved there. With the improved understanding of the degree distribution, as in Proposition
5.1
, the above result can be obtained, which allows for finer asymptotics to be proved.

Proof of Lemma
5.8
subject to Proposition
5.1
.

The first result immediately follows from a union bound and the fact that

(5.25)

n\mathbb{P}\!\left(\mathcal{Z}_{n}(v_{1})\geq k_{n}\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{P}\!\left(\mathcal{Z}_{n}(i)\geq k_{n}\right).

For the second result, let
A_{n,i}:=\{\mathcal{Z}_{n}(i)\geq k_{n}\},i\in[n]
. Then, by the Chung-Erdős inequality (which is one way of formalizing the second moment method),

(5.26)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(\max_{i\in[n]}\mathcal{Z}_{n}(i)\geq k_{n}\right)=\mathbb{P}\!\left(\cup_{i=1}^{n}A_{n,i}\right)\geq\frac{\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{P}\!\left(A_{n,i}\right)\Big)^{2}}{\sum_{i\neq j}\mathbb{P}\!\left(A_{n,i}\cap A_{n,j}\right)+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{P}\!\left(A_{n,i}\right)}.

By (
5.25
) it follows that
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{P}\!\left(A_{n,i}\right)=n\mathbb{P}\!\left(\mathcal{Z}_{n}(v_{1})\geq k_{n}\right)
. Furthermore, by Proposition
5.1
,

(5.27)

\sum_{i\neq j}\mathbb{P}\!\left(A_{n,i}\cap A_{n,j}\right)=n(n-1)\mathbb{P}\!\left(A_{n,v_{1}}\cap A_{n,v_{2}}\right)=(n\mathbb{P}\!\left(A_{n,v_{1}}\right))^{2}(1+o(1)),

where
v_{2}
is another vertex selected uniformly at random, unequal to
v_{1}
. Note that the condition that
k_{n}<c\log n
is required for this to hold. Together with the above lower bound, these two observations yield

(5.28)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(\cup_{i=1}^{n}A_{n,i}\right)\geq\frac{(n\mathbb{P}\!\left(A_{n,v_{1}}\right))^{2}}{(n\mathbb{P}\!\left(A_{n,v_{1}}\right))^{2}(1+o(1))+n\mathbb{P}\!\left(A_{n,v_{1}}\right)}=\frac{n\mathbb{P}\!\left(A_{n,v_{1}}\right)}{n\mathbb{P}\!\left(A_{n,v_{1}}\right)(1+o(1))+1}.

Hence, when
n\mathbb{P}\!\left(A_{n,v_{1}}\right)=n\mathbb{P}\!\left(\mathcal{Z}_{n}(v_{1})\geq k_{n}\right)
diverges with
n
, we obtain the desired result.
∎

5.2.
Non-rigorous proof of Proposition
5.1
when
k=1

What remains for this section is to prove Proposition
5.1
. As the proof is rather long and involved, we first provide a non-rigorous step-by-step proof of the case
k=1
. That is, we provide an asymptotic estimate for
\mathbb{P}\!\left(\mathcal{Z}_{n}(v)=m\right)
, where
v
is a vertex selected uniformly at random. Though this part is not essential for the proof of Proposition
5.1
, it helps the reader in understanding the complete proof of the proposition later in this section. In the next sub-section, we discuss the strategy of the full proof and take care of certain parts of the proof in separate lemmas. Whilst proving these lemmas, we refer back to the simpler non-rigorous proof provided here for guidance.
The main part of the proof is dedicated to proving (
5.1
). Once this is established, (
5.2
) follows without much effort. We thus focus on discussing the proof of (
5.1
) here first.

To provide a non-rigorous proof of the asymptotic estimate of
\mathbb{P}\!\left(\mathcal{Z}_{n}(v)=m\right)
, we first make the following simplification: we assume that
S_{\ell}=(\ell+1)\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]
for all
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
. Naturally, the law of large numbers implies that
S_{\ell}/\ell\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits^{a.s.}}\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]
, so that, up to lower-order error terms, this equality holds. Using this simplification, however, allows us to omit many details and focus on the most important steps of the proof.

Let us fix an
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, which we shall choose sufficiently small later. We then first condition on the value of the uniform vertex
v
, and distinguish between its values being smaller or larger than
n^{\varepsilon}
. That is,

(5.29)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\!\left(\mathcal{Z}_{n}(v)=m\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{P}_{W}\!\left(\mathcal{Z}_{n}(j)=m\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{1\leq j<n^{\varepsilon}}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{P}_{W}\!\left(\mathcal{Z}_{n}(j)=m\right)\right]+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{n^{\varepsilon}\leq j\leq n}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{P}_{W}\!\left(\mathcal{Z}_{n}(j)=m\right)\right].

We first take care of the second sum, and start by determining
\mathbb{P}_{W}\!\left(\mathcal{Z}_{n}(j)=m\right)
when
j\geq n^{\varepsilon}
, under the simplification that
S_{\ell}=(\ell+1)\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]
. Since
\mathcal{Z}_{n}(j)
is a sum of independent indicator random variables, we will see that
\mathcal{Z}_{n}(j)
is well-approximated by a Poisson random variable, whose rate equals the sum of the success probabilities of the indicator random variables. By the simplification
\mathcal{S}_{\ell}=(\ell+1)\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]
, we can thus determine that

(5.30)

\mathcal{Z}_{n}(j)=\sum_{i=j+1}^{n}\mathbbm{1}_{\{i\to j\}}=\sum_{i=j+1}^{n}\text{Ber}\Big(\frac{W_{j}}{S_{i-1}}\Big)\approx\text{Poi}\Big(\sum_{j=i+1}^{n}\frac{W_{j}}{i\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]}\Big)\approx\text{Poi}\Big(\frac{W_{j}}{\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]}\log(n/j)\Big)=:P_{j}.

It thus follows that
\mathbb{P}_{W}\!\left(\mathcal{Z}_{n}(j)=m\right)\approx\mathbb{P}_{W}\!\left(P_{j}=m\right)
. We now provide the necessary steps to obtain this intuitive result. The degree of vertex
j
in
T_{n}
equals exactly
m
when there exist vertices
i_{1},\ldots,i_{m}
that connect to
j
, and all other vertices do not. Since these connections are independent,

(5.31)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{W}\!\left(\mathcal{Z}_{n}(j)=m\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j<i_{1}<\ldots<i_{m}\leq n}\prod_{s=1}^{m}\frac{W_{j}}{S_{i_{s}-1}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}s=j+1\\
s\neq i_{\ell},\ell\in[m]\end{subarray}}^{n}\Big(1-\frac{W_{j}}{S_{s-1}}\Big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j<i_{1}<\ldots<i_{m}\leq n}\prod_{s=1}^{m}\frac{W_{j}}{i_{s}\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}s=j+1\\
s\neq i_{\ell},\ell\in[m]\end{subarray}}^{n}\Big(1-\frac{W_{j}}{s\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]}\Big)

Here, we sum over all possible choices of vertices
i_{1}<\ldots<i_{m}
that connect to
j
, and the final step follows from the simplifying assumption. We now include the terms
s\in\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{m}\}

in the second product, by changing the denominator in the fraction in the first product to
i_{s}\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]-W_{j}
. This yields

(5.32)

\mathbb{P}_{W}\!\left(\mathcal{Z}_{n}(j)=m\right)=\sum_{j<i_{1}<\ldots<i_{m}\leq n}\prod_{s=1}^{m}\frac{W_{j}}{i_{s}\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]-W_{j}}\prod_{s=j+1}^{n}\Big(1-\frac{W_{j}}{s\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]}\Big).

Since
W_{j}\in(0,1]
almost surely,
i_{s}>j\geq n^{\varepsilon}
, and
m<c\log n
, it follows that for any
\xi\in(0,1)
,

(5.33)

\prod_{s=1}^{m}\frac{1}{i_{s}\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]-W_{j}}=\prod_{s=1}^{m}\frac{1}{i_{s}\mathbb{E}\left[W\right](1+\mathcal{O}(n^{-\varepsilon}))}=(1+\mathcal{O}(n^{-(1-\xi)\varepsilon}))\prod_{s=1}^{m}\frac{1}{i_{s}\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]}.

Similarly,

(5.34)

\prod_{s=j+1}^{n}\Big(1-\frac{W_{j}}{s\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]}\Big)=\exp\Big(\sum_{s=j+1}^{n}\log\Big(1-\frac{W_{j}}{s\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]}\Big)\Big)=\exp\Big(-(1+\mathcal{O}(n^{-\varepsilon}))\sum_{s=j+1}^{n}\frac{W_{j}}{s\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]}\Big).

As

(5.35)

\sum_{s=j+1}^{n}\frac{1}{s}\approx\log(n/j),

we arrive at

(5.36)

\prod_{s=j+1}^{n}\Big(1-\frac{W_{j}}{s\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]}\Big)\approx\Big(\frac{j}{n}\Big)^{(1+\zeta_{n})W_{j}/\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]},

where
\zeta_{n}=\mathcal{O}(n^{-\varepsilon})
. Combined with (
5.33
) in (
5.32
), this yields

(5.37)

\mathbb{P}_{W}\!\left(\mathcal{Z}_{n}(j)=m\right)=(1+\mathcal{O}(n^{-(1-\xi)\varepsilon}))\Big(\frac{j}{n}\Big)^{(1+\zeta_{n})W_{j}/\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]}\Big(\frac{W_{j}}{\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]}\Big)^{m}\sum_{j<i_{1}<\ldots<i_{m}\leq n}\prod_{s=1}^{m}\frac{1}{i_{s}}.

We can approximate the sum by the multiple integrals

(5.38)

\int_{j}^{n}\int_{x_{1}}^{n}\cdots\int_{x_{m-1}}^{n}\prod_{s=1}^{m}x_{s}^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}x_{m}\ldots\mathrm{d}x_{1}.

By Lemma
A.4
we obtain that this equals
(\log(n/j))^{m}/m!
. So, we finally have

(5.39)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{W}\!\left(\mathcal{Z}_{n}(j)=m\right)

\displaystyle\approx\Big(\frac{j}{n}\Big)^{W_{j}/\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]}\Big(\frac{W_{j}}{\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]}\log(n/j)\Big)^{m}\frac{1}{m!}

\displaystyle=\exp\Big(-\frac{W_{j}}{\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]}\log(n/j)\Big)\Big(\frac{W_{j}}{\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]}\log(n/j)\Big)^{m}\frac{1}{m!}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{W}\!\left(P_{j}=m\right),

as desired. Since all vertex-weights are i.i.d., when we take an expectation over the weights, we can in fact omit the index
j
from
W_{j}
and use a general random variable
W
instead, so that
P_{j}
has mean
\log(n/j)W/\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]
. Using this expression in the second sum in (
5.29
) approximately yields

(5.40)

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{n^{\varepsilon}\leq j\leq n}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{P}_{W}\!\left(P_{j}=m\right)\right].

To avoid confusion between different parametrisations, we say that a random variable
X
has a Gamma
(\alpha,\beta)
distribution for some
\alpha,\beta>0
when it has a probability density function
f:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
with

(5.41)

f(x)=\frac{\beta^{\alpha}}{\Gamma(\alpha)}x^{\alpha-1}\mathrm{e}^{-\beta x},\qquad x>0.

We now use the following duality between Poisson and gamma random variables. Let

G\sim\text{Gamma}(m,1)
be a gamma random variable for some integer
m
. Note that we can also interpret
G
as a sum of
m
independent rate one exponential random variables. Then, conditionally on
W
, the event
\{P_{j}=m\}
can be thought of as the event that in a rate one Poisson process exactly
m
particles have arrived before time
\log(n/j)W/\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]
. This is equivalent to the sum of the first
m
inter-arrival times (which are rate one exponentially distributed) being at most
\log(n/j)W/\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]
, and the sum of the first
m+1
inter-arrival times exceeding this quantity. As we mentioned, this sum of
m
rate one exponential random variables is, in law, identical to
G
. So, if we let
V
be the
m+1^{\text{st}}
inter-arrival time, independent of
G
, then

(5.42)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{W}\!\left(P_{j}=m\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{W}\!\left(G\leq\log(n/j)W/\mathbb{E}\left[W\right],G+V>\log(n/j)W/\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{W}\!\left(Y\leq\log(n/j),Y+\widetilde{V}>\log(n/j)\right).

where, conditionally on
W
, we have
Y\sim\text{Gamma}(m,W/\mathbb{E}\left[W\right])
and
\widetilde{V}\sim\text{Exp}(W/\mathbb{E}\left[W\right])
. In (
5.40
), this yields

(5.43)

\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{n^{\varepsilon}\leq j\leq n}\mathbb{P}_{W}\!\left(Y\leq\log(n/j),Y+\widetilde{V}>\log(n/j)\right)\right]\approx\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{P}_{W}\!\left(Y\leq\log(n/v),Y+\widetilde{V}>\log(n/v)\right)\right],

where we recall that
v
is a uniform element of
[n]
. Observe that
\log(n/v)\approx T
, where
T\sim\text{Exp}(1)
. As a result, the conditional probability can be expressed, using that
Y
can be viewed as a sum of
m
i.i.d. copies of
\widetilde{V}
and the memoryless property of exponential random variables, as

(5.44)

\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{P}_{W}\!\left(Y\leq T,Y+\widetilde{V}\geq T\right)\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]}{W+\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]}\Big(\frac{W}{W+\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]}\Big)^{m}\right],

as desired. We provide some more details for this derivation. First, we write the sum as

(5.45)

\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{n^{\varepsilon}\leq j\leq n}\mathbb{P}_{W}\!\left(Y\leq\log(n/j)\right)-\mathbb{P}_{W}\!\left(Y+\widetilde{V}\leq\log(n/j)\right)\right].

Since
Y+\widetilde{V}\sim\text{Gamma}(m+1,W/\mathbb{E}\left[W\right])
, it suffices to deal with the first conditional probability only. We can approximate the sum by

(5.46)

\displaystyle\sum_{n^{\varepsilon}\leq j\leq n}\mathbb{P}_{W}\!\left(Y\leq\log(n/j)\right)

\displaystyle\approx\int_{n^{\varepsilon}}^{n}\mathbb{P}_{W}\!\left(Y\leq\log(n/x)\right)\,\mathrm{d}x=n\int_{0}^{(1-\varepsilon)\log n}\mathrm{e}^{-y}\mathbb{P}_{W}\!\left(Y\leq y\right)\,\mathrm{d}y.

The second step follows from a variable transformation
y=\log(n/x)
. We write
\mathbb{P}_{W}\!\left(Y\leq y\right)=\int_{0}^{y}f_{Y|W}(x)\mathrm{d}x
, where
f_{Y|W}(x)
is as in (
5.41
) with
\alpha=m-1,\beta=W/\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]
, the conditional probability density function of
Y
, conditionally on
W
. Changing the order of integration yields

(5.47)

\displaystyle n{}

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{(1-\varepsilon)\log n}\mathrm{e}^{-x}f_{Y|W}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x-n^{\varepsilon}\mathbb{P}_{W}\!\left(Y\leq(1-\varepsilon)\log n\right)

\displaystyle=n\Big(\frac{W}{\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]+W}\Big)^{m}\mathbb{P}_{W}\!\left(Y^{\prime}\leq(1-\varepsilon)\log n\right)-n^{\varepsilon}\mathbb{P}_{W}\!\left(Y\leq(1-\varepsilon)\log n\right),

where
Y^{\prime}\sim\text{Gamma}(m,1+W/\mathbb{E}\left[W\right])
, conditionally on
W
. A similar result with
m+1
and
Y^{\prime\prime}\sim\text{Gamma}(m+1,1+W/\mathbb{E}\left[W\right])
instead of
m
and
Y^{\prime}
follows for the probability of the event
\{Y+\widetilde{V}\leq\log(n/j)\}
. We remark that, via the construction of the random variables
Y^{\prime}
and
Y^{\prime\prime}
using
Y
and
Y+\widetilde{V}
, respectively, they are defined on the same probability space and that in fact
Y^{\prime\prime}
stochastically dominates
Y^{\prime}
. As such, combining both in (
5.45
) approximately provides

(5.48)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg[\Big(\frac{W}{\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]+W}{}

\displaystyle\Big)^{m}\Big(\mathbb{P}_{W}\!\left(Y^{\prime}\leq(1-\varepsilon)\log n\right)-\frac{W}{\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]+W}\mathbb{P}_{W}\!\left(Y^{\prime\prime}\leq(1-\varepsilon)\log n\right)\Big)\bigg]

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle(1+\mathcal{O}(n^{-(1-\xi)\varepsilon}))\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]+W}\Big(\frac{W}{\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]+W}\Big)^{m}\mathbb{P}_{W}\!\left(Y^{\prime}\leq(1-\varepsilon)\log n\right)\right]

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}\left[\Big(\frac{W}{\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]+W}\Big)^{m}\frac{W}{\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]+W}\mathbb{P}_{W}\!\left(Y^{\prime}\leq(1-\varepsilon)\log n,Y^{\prime\prime}>(1-\varepsilon)\log n\right)\right].

Here, the second step follows from the fact that
Y^{\prime}\preceq Y^{\prime\prime}
. We finally show that this quantity equals

(5.49)

\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]+W}\Big(\frac{W}{\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]+W}\Big)^{m}\right](1+o(n^{-\beta})),

for some small
\beta>0
, when
\varepsilon
is sufficiently small, by proving that
\mathbb{P}_{W}\!\left(Y^{\prime}\leq(1-\varepsilon)\log n\right)
and
\mathbb{P}_{W}\!\left(Y^{\prime\prime}>(1-\varepsilon)\log n\right)
are sufficiently close to one and zero, respectively. We thus conclude that

(5.50)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{n^{\varepsilon}\leq j\leq n}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{P}_{W}\!\left(\mathcal{Z}_{n}(j)=m\right)\right]
