. With a similar duality between Poisson and gamma random variables, we obtain, as in (
5.84
),

(5.163)

\mathbb{P}_{W}\!\left(P(a_{\ell})\geq m_{\ell}\right)=\mathbb{P}_{W}\!\left(Y_{\ell}\leq\log(n/j_{\ell})\right),

where
Y_{\ell}\sim\text{Gamma}(m_{\ell},a_{\ell})
, conditionally on
W_{\ell}
. Then, by the choice of
\zeta_{n}
, it follows that
((1+\zeta_{n})/(1-2\zeta_{n}))^{m_{\ell}}=1+\mathcal{O}\big(n^{-\delta\varepsilon}\log n\big)
. Using both these results in (
5.160
), we arrive at

(5.164)

\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\sum_{n^{\varepsilon}\leq j_{\ell}\leq n}\mathbb{P}_{W}\!\left(Y_{\ell}\leq\log(n/j_{\ell})\right)\Bigg]\Big(1+\mathcal{O}\big(n^{-\delta\varepsilon}\log n\big)\Big).

As the conditional probability is decreasing in
j_{\ell}
, we can bound the sum from above by an integral almost surely to obtain, as in (
5.86
) and (
5.89
),

(5.165)

\int_{\lfloor n^{\varepsilon}\rfloor}^{n}\mathbb{P}_{W}\!\left(Y_{\ell}\leq\log(n/x)\right)\,\mathrm{d}x\leq n\mathbb{E}_{W}[\mathrm{e}^{-Y_{\ell}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{Y_{\ell}\leq\log(n/\lfloor n^{\varepsilon}\rfloor)\}}]\leq n\Big(\frac{a_{\ell}}{1+a_{\ell}}\Big)^{m_{\ell}}.

Combining this almost sure upper bound with (
5.164
) in (
5.159
), we arrive at

(5.166)

n^{k-|S|}\prod_{\ell\in[k]\backslash S}\mathbb{E}\left[\Big(\frac{a_{\ell}}{1+a_{\ell}}\Big)^{m_{\ell}}\right]\Big(1+\mathcal{O}\big(n^{-\delta\varepsilon}\log n\big)\Big).

Finally, with the same steps as in (
5.92
), we obtain

(5.167)

n^{k-|S|}\prod_{\ell\in[k]\backslash S}\mathbb{E}\left[\Big(\frac{W}{\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]+W}\Big)^{m_{\ell}}\right]\big(1+o\big(n^{-\beta}\big)\big),

for some small
\beta>0
. Using this bound in (
5.155
) yields, for some constant
K>0
, the upper bound

(5.168)

K\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\subseteq[k]\\
|S|=i\end{subarray}}n^{C(S)(1+o(1))}\prod_{\ell\in[k]\backslash S}\mathbb{E}\left[\Big(\frac{W}{\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]+W}\Big)^{m_{\ell}}\right]=K\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\subseteq[k]\\
|S|=i\end{subarray}}n^{C(S)(1+o(1))}\prod_{\ell\in[k]\backslash S}p_{\geq m_{\ell}}.

By Remark
5.4
, the tail probability
p_{\geq m_{\ell}}=\mathcal{O}(p_{m_{\ell}})
and by (
5.146
) we have

n^{C(S)(1+o(1))}=o\big(n^{-\eta(S)}\prod_{\ell\in S}p_{m_{\ell}}\big)
for some
\eta(S)>0
. Combined, this yields

(5.169)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(n)_{k}}\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\sum_{\textbf{j}\in I_{n}(\varepsilon,i)}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{E^{(3)}_{n}}\mathbbm{1}_{E_{n}^{(1)}}\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\mathbb{P}_{W}\!\left(\mathcal{Z}_{n}(j_{\ell})\geq m_{\ell}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq K\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\subseteq[k]\\
|S|=i\end{subarray}}n^{C(S)(1+o(1))}\prod_{\ell\in[k]\backslash S}p_{\geq m_{\ell}}

\displaystyle=o\big(n^{-\widetilde{\eta}}\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}p_{m_{\ell}}\big),

with

(5.170)

\widetilde{\eta}:=\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\subseteq[k]\\
1\leq|S|\leq k-1\end{subarray}}\Big(C(S)-\sum_{\ell\in S}\log(\theta+\xi)c_{\ell}\Big),

which is strictly positive when
\varepsilon
and
\xi
are sufficiently small, similar to what is discussed above. Combining this with the fact that
\mathbb{P}\!\left(\big(E^{(1)}_{n}\cap E^{(3)}_{n}\cap E^{(4)}_{n}\cap E^{(5)}_{n}\big)^{c}\right)=o\big(n^{-\beta}\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}p_{m_{\ell}}\big)
uniformly in
m_{1},\ldots,m_{k}<c\log n
for some
\beta>0
by Lemma
A.2
, and the result in (
5.150
), we finally conclude,

(5.171)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(n)_{k}}\sum_{\textbf{j}\in I_{n}(\varepsilon)}\!\!\!\mathbb{P}\!\left(\mathcal{Z}_{n}(j_{\ell})=m_{\ell}\text{ for all }\ell\in[k]\right)={}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(n)_{k}}\sum_{\textbf{j}\in I_{n}(\varepsilon,k)}\mathbb{P}\!\left(\mathcal{Z}_{n}(j_{\ell})=m_{\ell}\text{ for all }\ell\in[k]\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{(n)_{k}}\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\sum_{\textbf{j}\in I_{n}(\varepsilon,i)}\!\!\!\mathbb{P}\!\left(\mathcal{Z}_{n}(j_{\ell})=m_{\ell}\text{ for all }\ell\in[k]\right)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle o\Big(n^{-\beta}\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}p_{m_{\ell}}\Big),

for some
\beta>0
in the case that
m_{\ell}=c_{\ell}\log n+o(\log n)
with
c_{\ell}\in[1/\log\theta,\theta/(\theta-1))
for all
\ell\in[k]
as well.

When the
m_{\ell}
do not all behave the same, that is, for some
\ell\in[k]

c_{\ell}\in[0,1/\log\theta)
and for some
c_{\ell}\in[1/\log\theta,\theta/(\theta-1))
, we can use a combination of the approaches outlined for either of the cases, which concludes the proof.
∎

We now discuss the required adaptations so that the proof holds for the model with
random out-degree
as well. This follows from the fact that Lemma
5.10
holds for this model, together with the fact that the degrees are still negatively quadrant dependent when the out-degree is random. As a result, all probabilities related to the degrees of multiple vertices can either be dealt with using Lemma
5.10
or can be split as a product of probabilities of individual vertices. From the perspective of the in-degree of an individual vertex
i\in[n]
, the model with out-degree one and the model with random out-degree are equivalent, as in every step the in-degree of vertex
i
increases by one with the same probability. Hence, the proof follows through in exactly the same way and Lemma
5.11
holds for the model with random out-degree as well.

5.6.
Proof of Proposition
5.1

We finally prove Proposition
5.1
, using Lemmas
5.10
and
5.11
. We remark that the proof does not use that the out-degree is deterministic but uses (
5.1
) only, so that the proof and hence (
5.2
) hold for both the model with fixed and
random out-degree
.

Proof of Proposition
5.1
.

As discussed before, (
5.1
) directly follows from (
5.58
) combined with Lemmas
5.10
and
5.11
. Using (
5.1
), we then prove (
5.2
). For ease of writing, we recall that

(5.172)

p_{k}:=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\theta-1}{\theta-1+W}\Big(\frac{W}{\theta-1+W}\Big)^{k}\right],\quad\text{and}\quad\;p_{\geq k}:=\mathbb{E}\left[\Big(\frac{W}{\theta-1+W}\Big)^{k}\right].

We start by assuming that
m_{\ell}=c_{\ell}\log n+o(\log n)
with
c_{\ell}\in(0,c)
for each
\ell\in[k]
. We discuss how to adjust the proof when
m_{\ell}=o(\log n)
for some or all
\ell\in[k]
at the end.

For each
\ell\in[k]
take an
\eta_{\ell}\in(0,c-c_{\ell})
so that
\lceil(1+\eta_{\ell})m_{\ell}\rceil<c\log n
. Then, we use the upper bound

(5.173)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}({}

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{n}(v_{\ell})\geq m_{\ell}\text{ for all }\ell\in[k])

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\sum_{j_{1}=m_{1}}^{\lfloor(1+\eta_{1})m_{1}\rfloor}\cdots\sum_{j_{k}=m_{k}}^{\lfloor(1+\eta_{k})m_{k}\rfloor}\mathbb{P}\!\left(\mathcal{Z}_{n}(v_{\ell})=j_{\ell}\text{ for all }\ell\in[k]\right)

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathbb{P}\!\left(\mathcal{Z}_{n}(v_{i})\geq\lceil(1+\eta_{i})m_{i}\rceil,\mathcal{Z}_{n}(v_{\ell})\geq m_{\ell}\text{ for all }\ell\neq i\right).

We first discuss the first term on the right-hand side. As (
5.1
) holds uniformly in
m_{1},\ldots,m_{k}

<c\log n
, we find

(5.174)

\displaystyle\sum_{j_{1}=m_{1}}^{\lfloor(1+\eta_{1})m_{1}\rfloor}\!\!\!\!\cdots\!\!\!\!\sum_{j_{k}=m_{k}}^{\lfloor(1+\eta_{k})m_{k}\rfloor}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\mathbb{P}\!\left(\mathcal{Z}_{n}(v_{\ell})=j_{\ell}\text{ for all }\ell\in[k]\right)

\displaystyle=\!\!\!\sum_{j_{1}=m_{1}}^{\lfloor(1+\eta_{1})m_{1}\rfloor}\!\!\!\!\cdots\!\!\!\!\sum_{j_{k}=m_{k}}^{\lfloor(1+\eta_{k})m_{k}\rfloor}\!\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}p_{j_{\ell}}\big(1+o\big(n^{-\beta}\big)\big)

\displaystyle=\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big(p_{\geq m_{\ell}}-p_{\geq\lceil(1+\eta_{\ell})m_{\ell}\rceil}\big)\big(1+o\big(n^{-\beta}\big)\big)

\displaystyle\leq\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}p_{\geq m_{\ell}}\big(1+o\big(n^{-\beta}\big)\big).

To finish the upper bound, it remains to show that the term on the second line of (
5.173
) can be incorporated in the
o\big(n^{-\beta}\big)
term, and it suffices to show this can be done for each term in the sum, independent of the value of
i
. Using the negative quadrant dependence of the degrees under the conditional probability measure
\mathbb{P}_{W}
(see Remark
5.2
and
[
15
, Lemma
7.1
]
), we find

(5.175)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{Z}_{n}(v_{i}){}

\displaystyle\geq\lceil(1+\eta_{i})m_{i}\rceil,\mathcal{Z}_{n}(v_{\ell})\geq m_{\ell}\text{ for all }\ell\in[k]\backslash\{i\})

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(n)_{k}}\sum_{1\leq j_{1}\neq\ldots\neq j_{k}\leq n}\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbb{P}_{W}\!\left(\mathcal{Z}_{n}(j_{i})\geq\lceil(1+\eta_{i})m_{i}\rceil\right)\prod_{\ell\in[k]\backslash\{i\}}\mathbb{P}_{W}\!\left(\mathcal{Z}_{n}(j_{\ell})\geq m_{\ell}\right)\Big]

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(n)_{k}}\sum_{\textbf{j}\in I_{n}(\varepsilon)}\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbb{P}_{W}\!\left(\mathcal{Z}_{n}(j_{i})\geq\lceil(1+\eta_{i})m_{i}\rceil\right)\prod_{\ell\in[k]\backslash\{i\}}\mathbb{P}_{W}\!\left(\mathcal{Z}_{n}(j_{\ell})\geq m_{\ell}\right)\Big]

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{(n)_{k}}\sum_{n^{\varepsilon}\leq j_{1}\neq\ldots\neq j_{k}\leq n}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbb{P}_{W}\!\left(\mathcal{Z}_{n}(j_{i})\geq\lceil(1+\eta_{i})m_{i}\rceil\right)\prod_{\ell\in[k]\backslash\{i\}}\mathbb{P}_{W}\!\left(\mathcal{Z}_{n}(j_{\ell})\geq m_{\ell}\right)\Big].

The first term in the last step can be included in the little
o
term in (
5.174
) (even when considering
m_{i}
rather than
\lceil(1+\eta_{i})m_{i}\rceil
in the probability), as follows from computations similar to the ones in (
5.130
) through (
5.171
), combined with Remark
5.4
(which states that
p_{\geq k}=\mathcal{O}(p_{k})
). It remains to show that the same holds for the second term in the last step. Again, we use an argument similar to the steps performed in (
5.155
) through (
5.166
) to arrive at

(5.176)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(n)_{k}}{}

\displaystyle\sum_{n^{\varepsilon}\leq j_{1}\neq\ldots\neq j_{k}\leq n}\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbb{P}_{W}\!\left(\mathcal{Z}_{n}(j_{i})\geq\lceil(1+\eta_{i})m_{i}\rceil\right)\prod_{\ell\in[k]\backslash\{i\}}\mathbb{P}_{W}\!\left(\mathcal{Z}_{n}(j_{\ell})\geq m_{\ell}\right)\Big]

\displaystyle\leq Kp_{\geq\lceil(1+\eta_{i})m_{i}\rceil}\prod_{\ell\in[k]\backslash\{i\}}p_{\geq m_{\ell}},

for some positive constant
K
. By Lemma
5.5
we have the inequalities

(5.177)

p_{\geq\lceil(1+\eta_{i})m_{i}\rceil}\leq\theta^{-\lceil(1+\eta_{i})m_{i}\rceil}\leq\theta^{-m_{i}}\theta^{-\eta_{i}m_{i}},\quad\text{and}\quad p_{\geq m_{i}}\geq(\theta+\xi)^{-m_{i}},

for any
\xi>0
. As a result, taking
\xi\in(0,\theta(\theta^{\eta_{i}}-1))
and setting
\phi_{i}:=1-(1+\xi/\theta)\theta^{-\eta_{i}}>0
, we obtain

(5.178)

p_{\geq\lceil(1+\eta_{i})m_{i}\rceil}\leq(\theta+\xi)^{-m_{i}}\big((1+\xi/\theta)\theta^{-\eta_{i}}\big)^{m_{i}}\leq p_{\geq m_{i}}(1-\phi_{i})^{m_{i}}.

As
m_{i}=c_{i}\log n(1+o(1))
, it follows that
(1-\phi_{i})^{m_{i}}=n^{-c_{i}\log(1/(1-\phi_{i}))(1+o(1))}
, so that

(5.179)

\frac{1}{(n)_{k}}\sum_{n^{\varepsilon}\leq j_{1}\neq\ldots\neq j_{k}\leq n}\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbb{P}_{W}\!\left(\mathcal{Z}_{n}(j_{i})\geq\lceil(1+\eta_{i})m_{i}\rceil\right)\prod_{\ell\in[k]\backslash\{i\}}\mathbb{P}_{W}\!\left(\mathcal{Z}_{n}(j_{\ell})\geq m_{\ell}\right)\Big]

can be incorporated in the little
o
term in (
5.174
) for each
i\in[k]
when we take a

\beta^{\prime}<\beta\wedge\min_{i\in[k]}c_{i}\log(1/(1-\phi_{i}))
. This yields

(5.180)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(\mathcal{Z}_{n}(v_{\ell})\geq m_{\ell}\text{ for all }\ell\in[k]\right)\leq\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\mathbb{E}\left[\Big(\frac{W}{\theta-1+W}\Big)^{m_{\ell}}\right]\big(1+o\big(n^{-\beta^{\prime}}\big)\big).

For a lower bound, we can omit the second line of (
5.173
) and use (
5.174
) to immediately obtain

(5.181)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\!\left(\mathcal{Z}_{n}(v_{\ell})\geq m_{\ell}\text{ for all }\ell\in[k]\right)\geq{}

\displaystyle\sum_{j_{1}=m_{1}}^{\lfloor(1+\eta_{1})m_{1}\rfloor}\cdots\sum_{j_{k}=m_{k}}^{\lfloor(1+\eta_{k})m_{k}\rfloor}\mathbb{P}\!\left(\mathcal{Z}_{n}(v_{\ell})=j_{\ell}\text{ for all }\ell\in[k]\right)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big(p_{\geq m_{\ell}}-p_{\geq\lceil(1+\eta_{\ell})m_{\ell}\rceil}\big)\big(1+o\big(n^{-\beta}\big)\big).

Again using (
5.178
) yields
p_{\geq m_{\ell}}-p_{\geq\lceil(1+\eta_{\ell})m_{\ell}\rceil}=p_{\geq m_{\ell}}\big(1+o\big(n^{-\beta^{\prime}}\big)\big)
when we set

\beta^{\prime}<\beta\wedge\min_{i\in[k]}c_{i}\log(1/(1-\phi_{i}))
. Combined with (
5.180
) this yields (
5.2
) for the case
m_{\ell}=c_{\ell}\log n+o(\log n)
with
c_{\ell}\in(0,c)
for all
\ell\in[k]
.

When
c_{\ell}=0
and thus
m_{\ell}=o(\log n)
for some (or all)
i\in[k]
, a few minor alterations are required. Most importantly, we substitute the quantity
\lfloor(c/2)\log n\rfloor
for
\lfloor(1+\eta_{\ell})m_{\ell}\rfloor
in all the steps. With this, the upper bound in (
5.174
) follows directly. Similarly, the first term on the right-hand side of (
5.175
) can be included in the little
o
term in (
5.174
), as the computations required (which are similar to those in (
5.130
) through (
5.150
)) include the case
m_{\ell}=o(\log n)
. We again use the upper bound in (
5.176
) to obtain

(5.182)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(n)_{k}}{}

\displaystyle\sum_{n^{\varepsilon}\leq j_{1}\neq\ldots\neq j_{k}\leq n}\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbb{P}_{W}\!\left(\mathcal{Z}_{n}(j_{i})\geq\lceil(c/2)\log n\rceil\right)\prod_{\ell\in[k]\backslash\{i\}}\mathbb{P}_{W}\!\left(\mathcal{Z}_{n}(j_{\ell})\geq m_{\ell}\right)\Big]

\displaystyle\leq Kp_{\geq\lceil(c/2)\log n\rceil}\prod_{\ell\in[k]\backslash\{i\}}p_{\geq m_{\ell}}

\displaystyle=K\frac{p_{\geq\lceil(c/2)\log n\rceil}}{p_{\geq m_{i}}}\prod_{\ell\in[k]}p_{\geq m_{\ell}}.

By Lemma
5.5
, we obtain that the fraction is at most
\theta^{-(c/2)\log n}(\theta+\xi)^{m_{i}}\leq\theta^{-(c/4)\log n}=n^{-c\log(\theta)/4}
for all
n
sufficiently large, when
m_{i}=o(\log n)
. As a result, this term can be included in the
o(n^{-\beta})
term in (
5.174
) for any
i\in[k]
such that
m_{i}=o(\log n)
, which proves the upper bound.

For the lower bound, we again substitute
\lfloor(c/2)\log n\rfloor
for
\lfloor(1+\eta_{i})m_{i}\rfloor
when
m_{i}=o(\log n)
. In (
5.181
), this yields a term

(5.183)

(p_{\geq m_{i}}-p_{\geq\lceil(c/2)\log n\rceil})(1+o(n^{-\beta}))=p_{\geq m_{i}}\Big(1-\frac{p_{\geq\lceil(c/2)\log n\rceil}}{p_{\geq m_{i}}}\Big)(1+o(n^{-\beta})).

Again, the fraction is at most
n^{-\eta}
for some
\eta>0
, which proves the lower bound and concludes the proof.
∎

6.
Proofs of the main theorems

With the tools developed in Section
5
, in particular Propositions
5.1
and
5.6
and Lemma
5.8
, we now prove the main results formulated in Section
2
.

First, we prove the main result for high degree vertices when the vertex-weight distribution has an atom at one, as in the

(

Atom

)

case.

Proof of Theorem
2.5
.

The proof follows the same approach as
[
1
, Theorem
1.2
]
. For an integer subsequence
(n_{\ell})_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
\varepsilon_{n_{\ell}}\to\varepsilon
as
\ell\to\infty
, it suffices to prove that for any
i<i^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}
,

(6.1)

(X^{(n_{\ell})}_{i},X^{(n_{\ell})}_{i+1},\ldots,X^{(n_{\ell})}_{i^{\prime}-1},X^{(n_{\ell})}_{\geq i^{\prime}})\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits^{d}}(\mathcal{P}^{\varepsilon}(i),\mathcal{P}^{\varepsilon}(i+1),\ldots,\mathcal{P}^{\varepsilon}(i^{\prime}-1),\mathcal{P}^{\varepsilon}([i^{\prime},\infty)))\qquad\text{as }\ell\to\infty

holds. We obtain this via the convergence of the factorial moments of
X^{(n_{\ell})}_{i},\ldots,X^{(n_{\ell})}_{i^{\prime}-1},X^{(n_{\ell})}_{\geq i^{\prime}}
. Recall
r_{k}
from (
2.17
). By Proposition
5.6
, for any non-negative integers
a_{i},\ldots,a_{i^{\prime}}
,

(6.2)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\Big(X^{(n_{\ell})}_{\geq i^{\prime}}\Big)_{a_{i^{\prime}}}\prod_{k=i}^{i^{\prime}-1}\Big(X_{k}^{(n_{\ell})}\Big)_{a_{k}}\right]={}

\displaystyle\Big(q_{0}\theta^{-i^{\prime}+\varepsilon_{n_{\ell}}}\Big)^{a_{i^{\prime}}}\prod_{k=i}^{i^{\prime}-1}\Big(q_{0}(1-\theta^{-1})\theta^{-k+\varepsilon_{n_{\ell}}}\Big)^{a_{k}}

\displaystyle\times\big(1+\mathcal{O}\big(r_{\lfloor\log_{\theta}n_{\ell}\rfloor+i}\vee n_{\ell}^{-\beta}\big)\big)

\displaystyle\to{}

\displaystyle\Big(q_{0}\theta^{-i^{\prime}+\varepsilon}\Big)^{a_{i^{\prime}}}\prod_{k=i}^{i^{\prime}-1}\Big(q_{0}(1-\theta^{-1})\theta^{-k+\varepsilon}\Big)^{a_{k}},

as
\ell\to\infty
. By using the properties of Poisson processes, it follows that the limit equals

(6.3)

\mathbb{E}\left[\big(\mathcal{P}^{\varepsilon}[i^{\prime},\infty)\big)_{a_{i^{\prime}}}\prod_{k=i}^{i^{\prime}-1}\big(\mathcal{P}^{\varepsilon}(k)\big)_{a_{k}}\right],

due to the particular form of the intensity measure of the Poisson process
\mathcal{P}
(which is used in the definition of the Poisson process
\mathcal{P}^{\varepsilon}
). The result then follows from
[
13
, Theorem
6.10
]
.
∎

For the results for the

(

Weibull

)

and

(

Gumbel

)

cases, as outlined in Theorems
2.6
and
2.7
, respectively, we combine the asymptotic behaviour of
p_{\geq k}
in Theorem
5.3
with Proposition
5.1
and Lemma
5.8
.

Proof of Theorem
2.6
.

To establish the convergence in probability, it follows from Lemma
5.8
that we need only consider
n\mathbb{P}\!\left(\mathcal{Z}_{n}(v_{1})\geq k_{n}\right)
for some adequate integer-valued
k_{n}
such that
k_{n}<c\log n
for some
c\in(0,\theta/(\theta-1))
and where
v_{1}
is a vertex selected from
[n]
uniformly at random. By Proposition
5.1
, this quantity equals
np_{\geq k_{n}}(1+o(1))
. Then, we use Theorem
5.3
and Remark
5.4
to obtain that, when
W
satisfies the

(

Weibull

)

case in Assumption
2.3
, this quantity is at most

(6.4)

n\overline{C}\,\overline{L}(k_{n})k_{n}^{-(\alpha-1)}\theta^{-k_{n}},

where
\overline{C}>1
is a constant. Now fix an arbitrary
\eta>0
and set
k_{n}:=\lfloor\log_{\theta}n-(\alpha-1)(1-\eta)\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n\rfloor
. This yields

(6.5)

\displaystyle n\overline{C}{}

\displaystyle\overline{L}(\log_{\theta}n(1+o(1)))(\log_{\theta}n)^{-(\alpha-1)}\theta^{-\lfloor\log_{\theta}n-(\alpha-1)(1-\eta)\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n\rfloor}(1+o(1))

\displaystyle\leq\overline{C}_{2}\overline{L}(\log_{\theta}n)(\log_{\theta}n)^{-(\alpha-1)}(\log_{\theta}n)^{(\alpha-1)(1-\eta)}

\displaystyle=\overline{C}_{2}\overline{L}(\log_{\theta}n)(\log_{\theta}n)^{-(\alpha-1)\eta}.

Here,
\overline{C}_{2}>0
is a suitable constant and we use that
k_{n}=\log_{\theta}n(1+o(1))
in the first step. Furthermore, we use
[
2
, Theorem
1.5.2
]
, which states that for a slowly-varying function
\overline{L}
,
\overline{L}(\lambda x)/\overline{L}(x)
converges to one uniformly in
\lambda
on any interval
[a,b]
such that
0<a\leq b<\infty
. As a result,
\overline{L}(\log_{\theta}n(1+o(1)))\leq c\overline{L}(\log_{\theta}n)
for some constant
c>1
and
n
sufficiently large. Finally, we use
[
2
, Proposition
1.3.6

(v)
]
to obtain that for any
\eta>0
, the final line of (
6.5
) tends to zero with
n
. This shows that for any
\eta>0
, with high probability,

(6.6)

\max_{j\in[n]}\frac{\mathcal{Z}_{n}(j)-\log_{\theta}n}{\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n}\leq-(\alpha-1)(1-\eta)

holds, due to the first result in Lemma
5.8
. A similar approach, when setting
k_{n}:=\lfloor\log_{\theta}n-(\alpha-1)(1+\eta)\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n\rfloor
, yields

(6.7)

n\mathbb{P}\!\left(\mathcal{Z}_{n}(v_{1})\geq k_{n}\right)\to\infty,

so that for any
\eta>0
, with high probability,

(6.8)

\max_{j\in[n]}\frac{\mathcal{Z}_{n}(j)-\log_{\theta}n}{\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n}\geq-(\alpha-1)(1+\eta)

holds. Together, these two bounds prove the desired result.
∎

Proof of Theorem
2.7
.

The proof of this theorem follows a similar approach to the proof of Theorem
2.6
. That is, we again apply the results from Theorem
5.3
together with the fact that

(6.9)

n\mathbb{P}\!\left(\mathcal{Z}_{n}(v_{1})\geq k_{n}\right)=np_{\geq k_{n}}(1+o(1)),

for some adequate integer-valued
k_{n}
such that
k_{n}<c\log n
for some
c\in(0,\theta/(\theta-1))
, as follows from Proposition
5.1
and Lemma
5.8
. In the

(

Gumbel

)

-
()
‣

(

Gumbel

)

sub-case, we know that

(6.10)

\displaystyle p_{\geq k_{n}}=\exp\Big(-\frac{\tau^{\gamma}}{1-\gamma}\Big(\frac{(1-\theta^{-1})k_{n}}{c_{1}}\Big)^{1-\gamma}(1+o(1))\Big)\theta^{-k_{n}},

where we recall that
\gamma=1/(\tau+1)
. To prove the desired results, we first set
k_{n}=\lfloor\log_{\theta}n-(1+\eta)C_{\theta,\tau,c_{1}}(\log_{\theta}n)^{1-\gamma}\rfloor
for any
\eta>0
, where we recall
C_{\theta,\tau,c_{1}}
from (
2.12
). Using this in (
6.10
) then yields

(6.11)

\displaystyle np_{\geq k_{n}}

\displaystyle=\frac{n}{\theta^{k_{n}}}\mathrm{e}^{-\log(\theta)C_{\theta,\tau,c_{1}}k_{n}^{1-\gamma}(1+o(1))}\geq\mathrm{e}^{\eta\log(\theta)C_{\theta,\tau,c_{1}}(\log_{\theta}n)^{1-\gamma}(1+o(1))},

where we use that
k_{n}^{1-\gamma}=(\log_{\theta}n)^{1-\gamma}(1+o(1))
in the last step. Hence,
n\mathbb{P}\!\left(\mathcal{Z}_{n}(v_{1})\geq k_{n}\right)
diverges. We thus conclude from Lemma
5.8
that

(6.12)

\max_{j\in[n]}\frac{\mathcal{Z}_{n}(j)-\log_{\theta}n}{(\log_{\theta}n)^{1-\gamma}}\geq-(1+\eta)C_{\theta,\tau,c_{1}}

holds with high probability. A similar approach, setting

k_{n}:=\lceil\log_{\theta}n-(1-\eta)C_{\theta,\tau,c_{1}}(\log_{\theta}n)^{1-\gamma}\rceil
and combining this with the first result of Lemma
5.8
yields

(6.13)

\max_{j\in[n]}\frac{\mathcal{Z}_{n}(j)-\log_{\theta}n}{(\log_{\theta}n)^{1-\gamma}}\leq-(1-\eta)C_{\theta,\tau,c_{1}}

holds with high probability. Together, these two bounds prove (
2.12
).

To prove (
2.13
) we apply the same methodology but use the asymptotic expression of
p_{k}
(and
p_{\geq k}
by adjusting constants), as in (
5.9
). We recall the constants
C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}
from (
2.14
) and set
k_{n}=:\lceil\log_{\theta}n-C_{1}(\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n)^{\tau}+C_{2}(\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n)^{\tau-1}\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n+(C_{3}+\eta)(\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n)^{\tau-1}\rceil
, for any
\eta>0
. Then, (
5.9
) yields

(6.14)

np_{\geq k_{n}}=\frac{n}{\theta^{k_{n}}}\exp\Big(-\Big(\frac{\log k_{n}}{c_{1}}\Big)^{\tau}\Big(1+\frac{\tau(\tau-1)\log\log k_{n}}{\log k_{n}}-\frac{K_{\tau,c_{1},\theta}}{\log k_{n}}(1+o(1))\Big)\Big).

Using Taylor expansions, we obtain

(6.15)

\displaystyle-\Big(\frac{\log k_{n}}{c_{1}}\Big)^{\tau}={}

\displaystyle-\Big(\frac{\log\log_{\theta}n}{c_{1}}\Big)^{\tau}+o(1)=-\log(\theta)C_{1}(\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n)^{\tau}+o(1),

\displaystyle\frac{\tau(\tau-1)}{c_{1}^{\tau}}(\log k_{n})^{\tau-1}\log\log k_{n}={}

\displaystyle\frac{\tau(\tau-1)}{c_{1}^{\tau}}(\log\log_{\theta}n)^{\tau-1}\log\log\log_{\theta}n+o(1)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\log(\theta)C_{2}(\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n)^{\tau-1}\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n

\displaystyle+(\log_{\theta}(\log\theta))(\log\theta)^{\tau}\frac{\tau(\tau-1)}{c_{1}^{\tau}}(\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n)^{\tau-1}+o(1),

\displaystyle-\frac{K_{\tau,c_{1},\theta}}{c_{1}^{\tau}}(\log k_{n})^{\tau-1}={}

\displaystyle-\frac{K_{\tau,c_{1},\theta}}{c_{1}^{\tau}}(\log\log_{\theta}n)^{\tau-1}+o(1)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle-(\log\theta)^{\tau-1}\frac{K_{\tau,c_{1},\theta}}{c_{1}^{\tau}}(\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n)^{\tau-1}+o(1),

where we recall
K_{\tau,c_{1},\theta}
from (
5.9
) in Theorem
5.3
. As a result,

(6.16)

\displaystyle n{}

\displaystyle\exp\Big(-\Big(\frac{\log k_{n}}{c_{1}}\Big)^{\tau}\!\!+\frac{\tau(\tau-1)}{c_{1}^{\tau}}(\log k_{n})^{\tau-1}\log\log k_{n}-\frac{K_{\tau,c_{1},\theta}}{c_{1}^{\tau}}(\log k_{n})^{\tau-1}(1+o(1))\Big)\theta^{-k_{n}}

\displaystyle=n\exp\big(-\log(\theta)C_{1}(\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n)^{\tau}+\log(\theta)C_{2}(\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n)^{\tau-1}\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n

\displaystyle+\log(\theta)C_{3}(\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n)^{\tau-1}(1+o(1))\big)\theta^{-k_{n}}

\displaystyle\leq\exp\big(-(\eta-o(1))(\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n)^{\tau-1}\big),

where in the last step we use that
k_{n}\geq\log_{\theta}n-C_{1}(\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n)^{\tau}+C_{2}(\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n)^{\tau-1}\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n+(C_{3}+\eta)(\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n)^{\tau-1}
. As the right-hand side tends to zero with
n
, Lemma
5.8
yields for any fixed
\eta>0
, with high probability,

(6.17)

\max_{i\in[n]}\frac{\mathcal{Z}_{n}(i)-\big(\log_{\theta}n-C_{1}(\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n)^{\tau}+C_{2}(\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n)^{\tau-1}\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n\big)}{(\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n)^{\tau-1}}\leq C_{3}+\eta.

With a similar approach, setting

(6.18)

k_{n}=\lfloor\log_{\theta}n-C_{1}(\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n)^{\tau}+C_{2}(\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n)^{\tau-1}\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n+(C_{3}-\eta)(\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n)^{\tau-1}\rfloor,

we can obtain that for any fixed
\eta>0
, with high probability,

(6.19)

\max_{i\in[n]}\frac{\mathcal{Z}_{n}(i)-\big(\log_{\theta}n-C_{1}(\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n)^{\tau}+C_{2}(\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n)^{\tau-1}\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n\big)}{(\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n)^{\tau-1}}\geq C_{3}-\eta.

Together these two bounds yield (
2.13
), which concludes the proof.
∎

Proof of Theorem
2.8
.

We first prove the asymptotic distribution of the maximum degree, whose proof follows the same approach as the proof of
[
1
, Theorem
1.3
]
. We need to consider two cases:
i_{n}=\mathcal{O}(1)
and
i_{n}\to\infty
such that
i_{n}+\log_{\theta}n<(\theta/(\theta-1))\log n
and
\liminf_{n\to\infty}i_{n}>-\infty
. For the former case, as
\exp(-q_{0}\theta^{-i_{n}+\varepsilon_{n}})=\mathcal{O}(1)
, it suffices to prove

(6.20)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(\max_{j\in[n]}\mathcal{Z}_{n}(j)\geq\lfloor\log_{\theta}n\rfloor+i_{n}\right)-(1-\exp(-q_{0}\theta^{-i_{n}+\varepsilon_{n}}))\to 0\qquad\text{as }n\to\infty.

By the definition of
X^{(n)}_{\geq i}
in (
2.8
), this is equivalent to

(6.21)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(X^{(n)}_{\geq i_{n}}=0\right)-\exp(-q_{0}\theta^{-i_{n}+\varepsilon_{n}})\to 0\qquad\text{as }n\to\infty.

This follows from Theorem
2.5
and the subsubsequence principle. That is, if we assume the convergence in (
6.21
) does not hold, then there exists a subsequence
(n_{\ell})_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
and a
\delta>0
such that

(6.22)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(X^{(n_{\ell})}_{\geq i_{n_{\ell}}}=0\right)-\exp(-q_{0}\theta^{-i_{n_{\ell}}+\varepsilon_{n_{\ell}}})>\delta\ \ \forall\,\ell\in\mathbb{N}.

However, as
\varepsilon_{n_{\ell}}
is bounded, there exists a subsubsequence
\varepsilon_{n_{\ell_{k}}}
such that
\varepsilon_{n_{\ell_{k}}}\to\varepsilon
for some
\varepsilon\in[0,1]
. Then, by Theorem
2.5
, the statement in (
6.22
) is false, from which the result follows.

In the latter case, we need only consider
\mathbb{E}\left[X^{(n)}_{\geq i_{n}}\right]
and
\mathbb{E}\left[\big(X^{(n)}_{\geq i_{n}}\big)_{2}\right]
, as by
[
3
, Corollary
1.11
]
,

(6.23)

\mathbb{E}\left[X^{(n)}_{\geq i_{n}}\right]-\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\big(X^{(n)}_{\geq i_{n}}\big)_{2}\right]\leq\mathbb{P}\!\left(X^{(n)}_{\geq i_{n}}>0\right)\leq\mathbb{E}\left[X^{(n)}_{\geq i_{n}}\right].

By Proposition
5.6
, we have that

(6.24)

\mathbb{E}\left[X^{(n)}_{\geq i_{n}}\right]=q_{0}\theta^{-i_{n}+\varepsilon_{n}}(1+o(1)),\qquad\mathbb{E}\left[\big(X^{(n)}_{\geq i_{n}}\big)_{2}\right]=\big(q_{0}\theta^{-i_{n}+\varepsilon_{n}}\big)^{2}(1+o(1)).

Hence, as
i_{n}\to\infty
and
\varepsilon_{n}
is bounded,

(6.25)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[X^{(n)}_{\geq i_{n}}\right]

\displaystyle=\big(1-\exp\big(-q_{0}\theta^{-i_{n}+\varepsilon_{n}}\big)\big)(1+o(1)),

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[X^{(n)}_{\geq i_{n}}\right]-\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\big(X^{(n)}_{\geq i_{n}}\big)_{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\big(1-\exp\big(-q_{0}\theta^{-i_{n}+\varepsilon_{n}}\big)\big)(1+o(1)).

Combining this with (
6.23
) yields the desired result.

Recall
\varepsilon_{n}:=\log_{\theta}n-\lfloor\log_{\theta}n\rfloor
. We now prove the limiting distribution of
|\mathcal{M}_{n_{\ell}}|
has the desired distribution, as in (
2.16
), when the subsequence
(n_{\ell})_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
is such that
\varepsilon_{n_{\ell}}\to\varepsilon\in[0,1]
, which follows the same approach as
[
7
, Theorem
1.1
]
. Consider the event
\mathcal{E}_{j,k}:=\{X^{(n_{\ell})}_{j}=k,X^{(n_{\ell})}_{\geq j+1}=0\}
for
j\in\mathbb{Z}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
\mathcal{E}_{j,k}
implies that there are exactly
k
vertices which attain the maximum degree
\lfloor\log_{\theta}n\rfloor+j
in the tree
T_{n_{\ell}}
. We observe that the events
\mathcal{E}_{j,k}
are pairwise disjoint for different values of
j
. As a result, we obtain the following inequalities: For any
M\in\mathbb{N}
,

(6.26)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\!\left(|\mathcal{M}_{n_{\ell}}|=k\right)=\mathbb{P}\bigg(\bigcup_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{E}_{j,k}\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\bigg(\bigcup_{j\leq-(M+1)}\mathcal{E}_{j,k}\bigg)+\sum_{j=-M}^{M-1}\mathbb{P}\!\left(\mathcal{E}_{j,k}\right)+\mathbb{P}\bigg(\bigcup_{j\geq M}\mathcal{E}_{j,k}\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\!\left(X^{(n_{\ell})}_{\geq-M}=0\right)+\sum_{j=-M}^{M-1}\mathbb{P}\!\left(\mathcal{E}_{j,k}\right)+\mathbb{P}\!\left(X^{(n_{\ell})}_{\geq M}>0\right),

and

(6.27)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(|\mathcal{M}_{n_{\ell}}|=k\right)=\mathbb{P}\bigg(\bigcup_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{E}_{j,k}\bigg)\geq\sum_{j=-M}^{M-1}\mathbb{P}\!\left(\mathcal{E}_{j,k}\right).

By Theorem
2.5
it thus follows that

(6.28)

\displaystyle\limsup_{n_{\ell}\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\!\left(|\mathcal{M}_{n_{\ell}}|=k\right)

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{M\in\mathbb{N}}\bigg(\mathbb{P}\!\left(X_{\geq-M}^{\varepsilon}=0\right)+\sum_{j=-M}^{M-1}\mathbb{P}\!\left(X_{j}^{\varepsilon}=k,X^{\varepsilon}_{\geq j}=0\right)+\mathbb{P}\!\left(X^{\varepsilon}_{\geq M}>0\right)\bigg),

\displaystyle\liminf_{n_{\ell}\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\!\left(|\mathcal{M}_{n_{\ell}}|=k\right)

\displaystyle\geq\limsup_{M\in\mathbb{N}}\sum_{j=-M}^{M}\mathbb{P}\!\left(X^{\varepsilon}_{j}=k,X^{\varepsilon}_{\geq j+1}=0\right),

where
X_{j}^{\varepsilon}\sim\text{Poi}\big(q_{0}(1-\theta^{-1})\theta^{-j+\varepsilon}\big)
and
X^{\varepsilon}_{\geq j}\sim\text{Poi}\big(q_{0}\theta^{-j+\varepsilon}\big)
are independent Poisson random variables, for
j\in\mathbb{Z}
. As a result,

(6.29)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\!\left(X_{\geq-M}^{\varepsilon}=0\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{-q_{0}\theta^{M+\varepsilon}},\qquad\mathbb{P}\!\left(X^{\varepsilon}_{\geq M}>0\right)=1-\mathrm{e}^{-q_{0}\theta^{-M+\varepsilon}},

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\!\left(X^{\varepsilon}_{j}=k,X^{\varepsilon}_{\geq j+1}=0\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\!\left(X^{\varepsilon}_{j}=k\right)\mathbb{P}\!\left(X^{\varepsilon}_{\geq j+1}=0\right)=\frac{1}{k!}\big(q_{0}(1-\theta^{-1})\theta^{-j+\varepsilon}\big)^{k}\mathrm{e}^{-q_{0}\theta^{-j+\varepsilon}}.

Hence, we obtain

(6.30)

\displaystyle\limsup_{n_{\ell}\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\!\left(|\mathcal{M}_{n_{\ell}}|=k\right)

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{M\in\mathbb{N}}\bigg(\mathrm{e}^{-q_{0}\theta^{M+\varepsilon}}\!\!+\!\sum_{j=-M}^{M-1}\!\frac{1}{k!}\big(q_{0}(1-\theta^{-1})\theta^{-j+\varepsilon}\big)^{k}\mathrm{e}^{-q_{0}\theta^{-j+\varepsilon}}\!+1-\mathrm{e}^{-q_{0}\theta^{-M+\varepsilon}}\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{k!}\big(q_{0}(1-\theta^{-1})\theta^{-j+\varepsilon}\big)^{k}\mathrm{e}^{-q_{0}\theta^{-j+\varepsilon}},

\displaystyle\liminf_{n_{\ell}\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\!\left(|\mathcal{M}_{n_{\ell}}|=k\right)

\displaystyle\geq\limsup_{M\in\mathbb{N}}\sum_{j=-M}^{M}\frac{1}{k!}\big(q_{0}(1-\theta^{-1})\theta^{-j+\varepsilon}\big)^{k}\mathrm{e}^{-q_{0}\theta^{-j+\varepsilon}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{k!}\big(q_{0}(1-\theta^{-1})\theta^{-j+\varepsilon}\big)^{k}\mathrm{e}^{-q_{0}\theta^{-j+\varepsilon}}.

It is then readily checked that the limit is indeed finite and that summing over all
k\in\mathbb{N}
yields one, which concludes the proof.
∎

Proof of Theorem
2.9
.

The proof follows the same argument as the proof of
[
1
, Theorem
1.4
]
, which is based on
[
3
, Theorem
1.24
]
. Let
1\leq a\leq b
be integers. Then, by Proposition
5.6
and since
i_{n}=o(\log n)
,

(6.31)

\mathbb{E}\left[\Big(X^{(n)}_{i_{n}}\Big)_{a}\right]-\Big(q_{0}(1-\theta^{-1})\theta^{-i_{n}+\varepsilon_{n}}\Big)^{a}=\mathcal{O}\big(\theta^{-i_{n}a}\big(r_{\lfloor\log_{\theta}n+i_{n}\rfloor}\vee n^{-\beta}\big)\big).

It then remains to show that the right-hand side is in fact
o(\theta^{i_{n}b})
. We note that
i_{n}=o(\log r_{\log_{\theta}n}\wedge\log n)
, so that we can write the right-hand side as

(6.32)

\mathcal{O}\big((r_{\log_{\theta}n})^{1-i_{n}a\log\theta/\log r_{\log_{\theta}n}}\vee n^{-\beta-i_{n}a\log\theta/\log n}\big)=\mathcal{O}\big((r_{\log_{\theta}n})^{1-o(1)}\vee n^{-\beta-o(1)}\big)=o(\theta^{i_{n}b}),

by the constraints on
i_{n}
, from which the result follows.
∎

7.
Technical details of examples

In this section we discuss some technical details of the examples discussed in Section
4
. In particular, for each example we provide a precise asymptotic expression of
p_{k}
and
p_{\geq k}
as well as a key element that leads to the results in Section
4
. That is, for each of the examples we state and prove the analogue of Proposition
5.6
. The three theorems presented in each of the examples in Section
4
mimic three of the theorems presented in Section
2
. That is, Theorems
4.2
and
4.6
are the analogue of Theorems
2.5
, Theorems
4.3
and
4.7
are the analogue of Theorem
2.8
, and Theorems
4.4
and
4.8
are the analogue of Theorem
2.9
. As a result, their proofs are very similar to the proofs of Theorems
2.5
,
2.8
, and
2.9
, so we omit proving the theorems stated in Section
4
.

7.1.
Example
4.1
, beta distribution

Let
W
be beta distributed, i.e. its distribution function satisfies (
4.1
) for some
\alpha,\beta>0
. We prove a result on (the tail of) the limiting degree distribution and provide additional building blocks required to prove the results in Example
4.1
.

Lemma 7.1
.

Let the distribution of
W
satisfy (
4.1
) for some
\alpha,\beta>0
and recall
p_{k},p_{\geq k}
from (
2.4
). Then,

(7.1)

\displaystyle p_{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma(\alpha+\beta)}{\Gamma(\alpha)}(1-\theta^{-1})^{1-\beta}k^{-\beta}\theta^{-k}\big(1+\mathcal{O}(1/k)\big),

\displaystyle p_{\geq k}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma(\alpha+\beta)}{\Gamma(\alpha)}(1-\theta^{-1})^{-\beta}k^{-\beta}\theta^{-k}\big(1+\mathcal{O}(1/k)\big).

Note that this lemma improves on the bounds in (
5.7
) by providing a precise multiplicative constant, rather than two slowly-varying functions that are (possibly) of different order.

Proof.

By the distribution of
W
, we immediately obtain that

(7.2)

\displaystyle p_{k}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}(\theta-1)x^{k}(\theta-1+x)^{-(k+1)}\frac{\Gamma(\alpha+\beta)}{\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(\beta)}x^{\alpha-1}(1-x)^{\beta-1}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=(\theta-1)^{-k}\frac{\Gamma(\alpha+\beta)}{\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(\beta)}\int_{0}^{1}x^{k+\alpha-1}(1-x)^{\beta-1}(1+x/(\theta-1))^{-(k+1)}\,\mathrm{d}x,

We now use Euler’s integral representation of the hypergeometric function. That is, for
a,b,c,z\in\mathbb{C}
such that
\mathrm{Re}(c)>\mathrm{Re}(b)>0
and
z
is not a real number greater than one,

(7.3)

\int_{0}^{1}x^{b-1}(1-x)^{c-b-1}(1-zx)^{-a}\,\mathrm{d}x=\frac{\Gamma(c-b)\Gamma(b)}{\Gamma(c)}{}_{2}F_{1}(a,b,c,z),

where
{}_{2}F_{1}
is the hypergeometric function. We thus obtain

(7.4)

(\theta-1)^{-k}\frac{\Gamma(\alpha+\beta)\Gamma(k+\alpha)}{\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(k+\alpha+\beta)}{}_{2}F_{1}(k+1,k+\alpha,k+\alpha+\beta,-1/(\theta-1)).

We then use one of the Euler transformations of the hypergeometric function,

(7.5)

{}_{2}F_{1}(a,b,c,z)=(1-z)^{c-a-b}{}_{2}F_{1}(c-a,c-b,c,z),

to arrive at

(7.6)

\theta^{-k}\frac{\Gamma(\alpha+\beta)\Gamma(k+\alpha)}{\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(k+\alpha+\beta)}\Big(\frac{\theta}{\theta-1}\Big)^{\beta-1}{}_{2}F_{1}(\alpha+\beta-1,\beta,k+\alpha+\beta,-1/(\theta-1)).

We directly find a particular case in which we can find the value of the hypergeometric function explicitly, namely when
\alpha+\beta=1
. When
\alpha+\beta=1
, we find that the hypergeometric function on the right-hand side of (
7.6
) equals one as the first argument equals zero, independent of the other arguments, so that we arrive at

(7.7)

\frac{(1-\theta^{-1})^{1-\beta}\Gamma(k+\alpha)}{\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(k+1)}\theta^{-k}=\frac{(1-\theta^{-1})^{1-\beta}}{\Gamma(\alpha)}k^{-\beta}\theta^{-k}\big(1+\mathcal{O}(1/k)\big),

since
\Gamma(x+a)/\Gamma(x)=x^{a}\big(1+\mathcal{O}(1/x)\big)
as
x\to\infty
and
\alpha=1-\beta
in this particular case. When
\alpha+\beta\neq 1
, we can obtain a similar expression. First, we use one of Pfaff’s transformations for the hypergeometric function,

(7.8)

{}_{2}F_{1}(a,b,c,z)=(1-z)^{-b}{}_{2}F_{1}(b,c-a,c,z/(z-1)).

Then, applying this to the right-hand side of (
7.6
), so that
z/(z-1)=1/\theta\in(-1,1)
, we can express the hypergeometric function as a power series. Namely, for
z
such that
|z|<1
,

(7.9)

{}_{2}F_{1}(a,b,c,z)=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{a^{(j)}b^{(j)}}{c^{(j)}}\frac{z^{j}}{\Gamma(j)},

where
a^{(j)}:=\prod_{\ell=1}^{j}(a+(\ell-1))
(and similarly for
b^{(j)},c^{(j)}
). Thus, combining the Pfaff transformation and the power series representation yields

(7.10)

{}_{2}F_{1}(\alpha+\beta-1,\beta,k+\alpha+\beta,-1/(\theta-1))=\Big(\frac{\theta}{\theta-1}\Big)^{-\beta}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{\beta^{(j)}(k+1)^{(j)}}{(k+\alpha+\beta)^{(j)}}\frac{\theta^{-j}}{j!}.

From the
\alpha+\beta=1
case, we immediately distil that

(7.11)

\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{\beta^{(j)}}{j!}\theta^{-j}=\Big(\frac{\theta}{\theta-1}\Big)^{\beta}.

The aim is to show that for
k
large, the series in (
7.10
) is close to
(\theta/(\theta-1))^{\beta}
for any choice of
\alpha,\beta>0
, so that the entire term in (
7.10
) is close to one. We consider two cases, namely
\alpha+\beta<1
and
\alpha+\beta>1
. Let us start with the latter. We immediately obtain the upper bound
(k+\alpha+\beta)^{(j)}>(k+1)^{(j)}
, so that using (
7.11
) yields that the right-hand side of (
7.10
) is at most one. For a lower bound, note that

(7.12)

\frac{(k+1)^{(j)}}{(k+\alpha+\beta)^{(j)}}=\prod_{\ell=1}^{j}\Big(1-\frac{\alpha+\beta-1}{k+\alpha+\beta+(\ell-1)}\Big)\geq\Big(1-\frac{\alpha+\beta-1}{k+\alpha+\beta}\Big)^{j},

as the fraction in the second step in decreasing in
\ell
, since
\alpha+\beta-1>0
. We thus have

(7.13)

\Big(\frac{\theta}{\theta-1}\Big)^{-\beta}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{\beta^{(j)}(k+1)^{(j)}}{(k+\alpha+\beta)^{(j)}}\frac{\theta^{-j}}{j!}\geq\Big(\frac{\theta}{\theta-1}\Big)^{-\beta}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{\beta^{(j)}}{j!}\Big(\Big(1-\frac{\alpha+\beta-1}{k+\alpha+\beta}\Big)\frac{1}{\theta}\Big)^{j},

which, using (
7.11
), equals

(7.14)

\Big(\frac{\theta-1}{\theta-1+\frac{\alpha+\beta-1}{k+\alpha+\beta}}\Big)^{\beta}=\Big(1-\frac{\alpha+\beta-1}{(\theta-1)(k+\alpha+\beta)+(\alpha+\beta-1)}\Big)^{\beta}=1-\mathcal{O}(1/k).

A similar approach can be used for
\alpha+\beta<1
, where one would have an elementary lower bound and an upper bound that is
1+\mathcal{O}(1/k)
. In total, combining the above in (
7.10
) and in (
7.6
) yields

(7.15)

p_{k}=\frac{\Gamma(\alpha+\beta)}{\Gamma(\alpha)}(1-\theta^{-1})^{1-\beta}k^{-\beta}\theta^{-k}\big(1+\mathcal{O}(1/k)\big).

An equivalent computation can be performed for

(7.16)

\int_{0}^{1}x^{k}(\theta-1+x)^{-k}\frac{\Gamma(\alpha+\beta)}{\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(\beta)}x^{\alpha-1}(1-x)^{\beta-1}\,\mathrm{d}x,

to obtain that it equals

(7.17)

\displaystyle\theta^{-k}{}

\displaystyle\frac{\Gamma(\alpha+\beta)\Gamma(k+\alpha)}{\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(k+\alpha+\beta)}\Big(\frac{\theta}{\theta-1}\Big)^{\beta}{}_{2}F_{1}(\alpha+\beta,\beta,k+\alpha+\beta,-1/(\theta-1))

\displaystyle=\theta^{-k}\frac{\Gamma(\alpha+\beta)\Gamma(k+\alpha)}{\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(k+\alpha+\beta)}{}_{2}F_{1}(\beta,k,k+\alpha+\beta,1/\theta)

\displaystyle=\theta^{-k}\frac{\Gamma(\alpha+\beta)\Gamma(k+\alpha)}{\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(k+\alpha+\beta)}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{\beta^{(j)}k^{(j)}}{(k+\alpha+\beta)^{(j)}}\frac{\theta^{-j}}{j!}.

In this case an equivalent approach for bounding the sum on the right-hand side works for all
\alpha,\beta>0
. Hence, for (
7.16
) we obtain the asymptotic expression

(7.18)

p_{\geq k}=\frac{\Gamma(\alpha+\beta)}{\Gamma(\alpha)}(1-\theta^{-1})^{-\beta}k^{-\beta}\theta^{-k}\big(1+\mathcal{O}(1/k)\big),

which concludes the proof.
∎

Recall that in this example we set

(7.19)

\displaystyle X^{(n)}_{i}

\displaystyle:=|\{j\in[n]:\mathcal{Z}_{n}(j)=\lfloor\log_{\theta}n-\beta\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n\rfloor+i\}|,

\displaystyle X^{(n)}_{\geq i}

\displaystyle:=|\{j\in[n]:\mathcal{Z}_{n}(j)\geq\lfloor\log_{\theta}n-\beta\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n\rfloor+i\}|,

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{n}

\displaystyle:=(\log_{\theta}n-\beta\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n)-\lfloor\log_{\theta}n-\beta\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n\rfloor.

We then state the analogue of Proposition
5.6
.

Proposition 7.2
.

Consider the WRT model as in Definition
2.1
with vertex-weights
(W_{i})_{i\in[n]}
whose distribution satisfies (
4.1
) for some
\alpha,\beta>0
, and recall
c_{\alpha,\beta,\theta}
from (
4.3
). For a fixed
K\in\mathbb{N},c\in(0,\theta/(\theta-1))
, the following holds. For any integer-valued
i_{n},i_{n}^{\prime},
such that
0<\log_{\theta}n+i_{n}<\log_{\theta}n+i_{n}^{\prime}<c\log n
and
i_{n},i_{n}^{\prime}=\delta\log_{\theta}n+o(\log n)
, for some
\delta\in(-1,c\log\theta-1)\cup\{0\}
and for
a_{i_{n}},\ldots,a_{i_{n}^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
satisfying
a_{i_{n}}+\cdots+a_{i_{n}^{\prime}}=K
,

(7.20)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg[\Big(X^{(n_{\ell})}_{\geq i_{n}^{\prime}}\Big)_{a_{i_{n}^{\prime}}}\prod_{k=i_{n}}^{i_{n}^{\prime}-1}\Big(X_{k}^{(n_{\ell})}\Big)_{a_{k}}\bigg]={}

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{c_{\alpha,\beta,\theta}}{(1+\delta)^{\beta}}\theta^{-i_{n}^{\prime}+\varepsilon_{n}}\Big)^{a_{i_{n}^{\prime}}}\prod_{k=i_{n}}^{i_{n}^{\prime}-1}\Big(\frac{c_{\alpha,\beta,\theta}(1-\theta^{-1})}{(1+\delta)^{\beta}}\theta^{-k+\varepsilon_{n}}\Big)^{a_{k}}

\displaystyle\times\Big(1+\mathcal{O}\Big(\frac{\log\log n}{\log n}\vee\frac{|i_{n}-\delta\log_{\theta}n|\vee|i_{n}^{\prime}-\delta\log_{\theta}n|}{\log n}\Big)\Big).

Proof.

Set
K^{\prime}:=K-a_{i_{n}^{\prime}}
and for each
i_{n}\leq k\leq i_{n}^{\prime}
and each
u
such that
\sum_{\ell=i_{n}}^{k-1}a_{\ell}<u\leq\sum_{\ell=i_{n}}^{k}a_{\ell}
, let
m_{u}=\lfloor\log_{\theta}n-\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n\rfloor+k
. Also, let
(v_{u})_{u\in[K]}
be
K
vertices selected uniformly at random without replacement from
[n]
. Then, as the
X^{(n)}_{\geq k}
and
X^{(n)}_{k}
can be expressed as sums of indicators, following the same steps as in the proof of Proposition
5.6
,

(7.21)

\mathbb{E}\left[\Big(X^{(n)}_{\geq i_{n}^{\prime}}\Big)_{a_{i_{n}^{\prime}}}\prod_{k=i_{n}}^{i_{n}^{\prime}-1}\Big(X_{k}^{(n)}\Big)_{a_{k}}\right]=(n)_{K}\sum_{\ell=0}^{K^{\prime}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\subseteq[K^{\prime}]\\
|S|=\ell\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\ell}\mathbb{P}\!\left(\mathcal{Z}_{n}(v_{u})\geq m_{u}+\mathbbm{1}_{\{u\in S\}}\text{ for all }u\in[K]\right).

By Proposition
5.1
,

(7.22)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(\mathcal{Z}_{n}(v_{\ell})\geq m_{u}+\mathbbm{1}_{\{u\in S\}}\text{ for all }u\in[K]\right)=\prod_{u=1}^{K}\mathbb{E}\left[\Big(\frac{W}{\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]+W}\Big)^{m_{u}+\mathbbm{1}_{\{u\in S\}}}\right](1+o(n^{-\beta})),

for some
\beta>0
. By Lemma
7.1
, when
|S|=\ell
,

(7.23)

\displaystyle\prod_{u=1}^{K}{}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\Big(\frac{W}{\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]+W}\Big)^{m_{u}+\mathbbm{1}_{\{u\in S\}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\Big(\frac{\Gamma(\alpha+\beta)}{\Gamma(\alpha)}(1-\theta^{-1})^{-\beta}\Big)^{K}\theta^{-\sum_{u=1}^{K}m_{u}-\ell}\prod_{u=1}^{K}(m_{u}+\mathbbm{1}_{\{u\in S\}})^{-\beta}(1+\mathcal{O}(1/\log n)).

Here, we are able to obtain the error term
1-\mathcal{O}(1/\log n)
due to the fact that
\log_{\theta}n+i_{n}>\eta\log n
for some
\eta\in(0,1+\delta)
when
n
is large. Moreover, as
i_{n},i_{n}^{\prime}=\delta\log_{\theta}n+o(\log n)
and thus
m_{u}\sim(1+\delta)\log_{\theta}n
for each
u\in[K]
,

(7.24)

\prod_{u=1}^{K}(m_{u}+\mathbbm{1}_{\{u\in S\}})^{-\beta}=((1+\delta)\log_{\theta}n)^{-\beta K}\Big(1+\mathcal{O}\Big(\frac{\log\log n}{\log n}\vee\frac{|i_{n}-\delta\log_{\theta}n|\vee|i_{n}^{\prime}-\delta\log_{\theta}n|}{\log n}\Big)\Big),

uniformly in
S
(and
\ell
). Recalling that
c_{\alpha,\beta,\theta}=(\Gamma(\alpha+\beta)/\Gamma(\alpha))(1-\theta^{-1})^{-\beta}
, we thus arrive at

(7.25)

\displaystyle(n)_{K}{}

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=0}^{K^{\prime}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\subseteq[K^{\prime}]\\
|S|=\ell\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\ell}\big(c_{\alpha,\beta,\theta}(1+\delta)^{-\beta}(\log_{\theta}n)^{-\beta}\big)^{K}\theta^{-\sum_{u=1}^{K}m_{u}-\ell}

\displaystyle\times\Big(1+\mathcal{O}\Big(\frac{\log\log n}{\log n}\vee\frac{|i_{n}-\delta\log_{\theta}n|\vee|i_{n}^{\prime}-\delta\log_{\theta}n|}{\log n}\Big)\Big)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\big(c_{\alpha,\beta,\theta}(1+\delta)^{-\beta}\theta^{-i_{n}^{\prime}+\varepsilon_{n}}\big)^{a_{i_{n}^{\prime}}}\prod_{k=i_{n}}^{i_{n}^{\prime}-1}\!\big(c_{\alpha,\beta,\theta}(1+\delta)^{-\beta}(1-\theta^{-1})\theta^{-k+\varepsilon_{n}}\big)^{a_{k}}

\displaystyle\times\Big(1+\mathcal{O}\Big(\frac{\log\log n}{\log n}\vee\frac{|i_{n}-\delta\log_{\theta}n|\vee|i_{n}^{\prime}-\delta\log_{\theta}n|}{\log n}\Big)\Big),

where the last step follows from a similar argument as in the proof of Proposition
5.6
.
∎

With Proposition
7.2
at hand, the proofs of Theorems
4.2
,
4.3
, and
4.4
follow the same approach as the proofs of Theorems
2.5
,
2.8
, and
2.9
, respectively.

7.2.
Example
4.5
, fraction of ‘gamma’ random variables

Let the distribution of
W
satisfy (
4.9
) for some
b\in\mathbb{R},c_{1}>0
with
bc_{1}\leq 1
. We prove a result on (the tail of) the limiting degree distribution and provide additional building blocks required to prove the results in Example
4.5
.

Lemma 7.3
.

Let the distribution of
W
satisfy (
4.9
) for some
b\in\mathbb{R},c_{1}>0
such that
bc_{1}\leq 1
, and recall
p_{k},p_{\geq k},
and
C
from (
2.4
) and (
4.12
), respectively. Then,

(7.26)

\displaystyle p_{k}

\displaystyle=(1-\theta^{-1})Ck^{b/2+1/4}\mathrm{e}^{-2\sqrt{c_{1}^{-1}(1-\theta^{-1})k}}\theta^{-k}\big(1+\mathcal{O}\big(1/\sqrt{k}\big)\big),

\displaystyle p_{\geq k}

\displaystyle=Ck^{b/2+1/4}\mathrm{e}^{-2\sqrt{c_{1}^{-1}(1-\theta^{-1})k}}\theta^{-k}\big(1+\mathcal{O}\big(1/\sqrt{k}\big)\big).

Note that this lemma improves on the bounds in (
5.8
) by providing a polynomial correction term and a precise multiplicative constant.

Proof.

We start by proving the equality for
p_{\geq k}
and then show the similar result for
p_{k}
. By (
4.9
), we obtain the following expression for
p_{\geq k}
.

(7.27)

\displaystyle p_{\geq k}={}

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}x^{k}(\theta-1+x)^{-k}c_{1}^{-1}(1-x)^{-(2+b)}\mathrm{e}^{-c_{1}^{-1}x/(1-x)}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{1}x^{k}(\theta-1+x)^{-k}b(1-x)^{-(1+b)}\mathrm{e}^{-c_{1}^{-1}x/(1-x)}\,\mathrm{d}x.

The second integral is of a similar form as the first, with a different constant in front and a different polynomial exponent. We hence only provide an explicit analysis of the first integral. Using a variable transform
u=x/(1-x)
, we find the first integral equals

(7.28)

\theta^{-k}c_{1}^{-1}\int_{0}^{\infty}u^{k}(1+u)^{b-k}\Big(1-\frac{1}{\theta(1+u)}\Big)^{-k}\mathrm{e}^{-c_{1}^{-1}u}\,\mathrm{d}u.

We now define
X_{u}
to be a negative binomial random variable with parameters
k
and
p_{u}:=(\theta(1+u))^{-1}
, for any
u>0
. As the sum over the probability mass function of
X_{u}
is one irrespectively of the value of
u
, we obtain that the above equals

(7.29)

\displaystyle\theta^{-k}c_{1}^{-1}{}

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\binom{j+k-1}{j}p_{u}^{j}(1-p_{u})^{k}u^{k}(1+u)^{b-k}\Big(1-\frac{1}{\theta(1+u)}\Big)^{-k}\mathrm{e}^{-c_{1}^{-1}u}\,\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle=\theta^{-k}c_{1}^{-1}\int_{0}^{\infty}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\binom{j+k-1}{j}\theta^{-j}u^{k}(1+u)^{b-(j+k)}\mathrm{e}^{-c_{1}^{-1}u}\,\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle=\theta^{-k}c_{1}^{-1}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\binom{j+k-1}{j}\theta^{-j}\Gamma(k+1)\,U(k+1,2+b-j,c_{1}^{-1}),

where
U(a,b,z)
is the confluent hypergeometric function of the second kind, defined as

(7.30)

U(a,b,z):=\frac{1}{\Gamma(a)}\int_{0}^{\infty}x^{a-1}(1+x)^{b-a-1}\mathrm{e}^{-zx}\,\mathrm{d}x,

whenever
\text{Re}(a)>0
. Using the Kummer transform
U(a,b,z)=z^{1-b}U(1+a-b,2-b,z)
yields

(7.31)

\theta^{-k}{}c_{1}^{-1}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\binom{j+k-1}{j}\theta^{-j}\Gamma(k+1)\,c_{1}^{b-(j-1)}U(j+k-b,j-b,c_{1}^{-1}).

Again using the definition of the confluent hypergeometric function of the second kind, we obtain

(7.32)

\displaystyle c_{1}^{b}\theta^{-k}{}

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{\Gamma(j+k)\Gamma(k+1)}{\Gamma(k)\Gamma(j+1)\Gamma(j+k-b)}(c_{1}\theta)^{-j}\int_{0}^{\infty}u^{j+k-b-1}(1+u)^{-(k+1)}\mathrm{e}^{-c_{1}^{-1}u}\,\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle c_{1}^{b}k\theta^{-k}\frac{\Gamma(k)}{\Gamma(k-b)}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{\Gamma(j+k)\Gamma(k-b)}{\Gamma(j+k-b)\Gamma(k)}\frac{1}{j!}(c_{1}\theta)^{-j}\int_{0}^{\infty}u^{j+k-b-1}(1+u)^{-(k+1)}\mathrm{e}^{-c_{1}^{-1}u}\,\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle c_{1}^{b}k\theta^{-k}\frac{\Gamma(k)}{\Gamma(k-b)}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{(k)^{(j)}}{(k-b)^{(j)}}\frac{1}{j!}(c_{1}\theta)^{-j}\int_{0}^{\infty}u^{j+k-b-1}(1+u)^{-(k+1)}\mathrm{e}^{-c_{1}^{-1}u}\,\mathrm{d}u,

where
(x)^{(j)}:=x(x+1)\cdots(x+(j-1))
, for any
x\in\mathbb{R}
and
j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. We can then bound

(7.33)

\frac{(k)^{(j)}}{(k-b)^{(j)}}\geq\begin{cases}1&\mbox{if }k>b\geq 0,\\
\Big(\frac{k}{k-b}\Big)^{j}&\mbox{if }b<0.\end{cases}\ \text{ and }\ \frac{(k)^{(j)}}{(k-b)^{(j)}}\leq\begin{cases}1&\mbox{if }b<0,\\
\Big(\frac{k}{k-b}\Big)^{j}&\mbox{if }k>b\geq 0.\end{cases}

As the bounds are symmetric, we can assume that
b\geq 0
without loss of generality; the other case follows similarly. We deal with the lower bound first. This yields

(7.34)

\displaystyle c_{1}^{b}{}

\displaystyle k\theta^{-k}\frac{\Gamma(k)}{\Gamma(k-b)}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{j!}(c_{1}\theta)^{-j}\int_{0}^{\infty}u^{j+k-b-1}(1+u)^{-(k+1)}\mathrm{e}^{-c_{1}^{-1}u}\,\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle=c_{1}^{b}k\theta^{-k}\frac{\Gamma(k)}{\Gamma(k-b)}\int_{0}^{\infty}u^{k-b-1}(1+u)^{-(k+1)}\mathrm{e}^{-c_{1}^{-1}(1-\theta^{-1})u}\,\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle=c_{1}^{b}k\theta^{-k}\frac{\Gamma(k)}{\Gamma(k-b)}\Gamma(k-b)U(k-b,-b,c_{1}^{-1}(1-\theta^{-1})).

It follows from
[
20
,
(3.12)
and
(3.15)
]
that, when
a>d/2
is large and
d,z
are bounded,

(7.35)

\Gamma(a)U(a,d,z^{2})=2\mathrm{e}^{z^{2}/2}\Big(\frac{2z}{u}\Big)^{1-d}K_{1-d}(uz)\big(1+\mathcal{O}(1/u)\big),

where
u=2\sqrt{a-d/2}
and
K_{1-d}(uz)
is a modified Bessel function. Combined with the asymptotic expression for the modified Bessel function as in
[
16
,
(10.40.2)
]
, we obtain

(7.36)

\Gamma(a)U(a,d,z^{2})=2\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2uz}}\mathrm{e}^{z^{2}/2-uz}\Big(\frac{2z}{u}\Big)^{1-d}\big(1+\mathcal{O}(1/u)\big).

In this particular case, it yields

(7.37)

\displaystyle\Gamma(k-b)

\displaystyle U(k-b,-b,c_{1}^{-1}(1-\theta^{-1}))

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{c_{1}^{-1}(1-\theta^{-1})/2}\sqrt{\pi}(c_{1}^{-1}(1-\theta^{-1}))^{1/4+b/2}k^{-b/2-3/4}\mathrm{e}^{-2\sqrt{c_{1}^{-1}(1-\theta^{-1})k}}\big(1+\mathcal{O}\big(1/\sqrt{k}\big)\big).

Using this, as well as
\Gamma(k)/\Gamma(k-b)=k^{b}(1+\mathcal{O}(1/k))
, in (
7.34
), we arrive at

(7.38)

\mathrm{e}^{c_{1}^{-1}(1-\theta^{-1})/2}\sqrt{\pi}c_{1}^{-1/4+b/2}((1-\theta^{-1})k)^{1/4+b/2}\mathrm{e}^{-2\sqrt{c_{1}^{-1}(1-\theta^{-1})k}}\theta^{-k}\big(1+\mathcal{O}\big(1/\sqrt{k}\big)\big).

We then tend to the upper bound in (
7.33
) for
b\geq 0
, which yields

(7.39)

\displaystyle c_{1}^{b}{}

\displaystyle k\theta^{-k}\frac{\Gamma(k)}{\Gamma(k-b)}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{j!}\Big(\frac{(c_{1}\theta)^{-1}k}{k-b}\Big)^{j}\int_{0}^{\infty}u^{j+k-b-1}(1+u)^{-(k+1)}\mathrm{e}^{-c_{1}^{-1}u}\,\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle=c_{1}^{b}k\theta^{-k}\frac{\Gamma(k)}{\Gamma(k-b)}\int_{0}^{\infty}u^{k-b-1}(1+u)^{-(k+1)}\mathrm{e}^{-(c_{1}^{-1}(1-\theta^{-1})-(c_{1}\theta)^{-1}b/(k-b))u}\,\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle=c_{1}^{b}k\theta^{-k}\frac{\Gamma(k)}{\Gamma(k-b)}\Gamma(k-b)U(k-b,-b,c_{1}^{-1}(1-\theta^{-1})-(c_{1}\theta)^{-1}b/(k-b)).

From the asymptotic result in (
7.36
) we find that

(7.40)

U\Big(k-b,-b,\frac{1}{c_{1}}\big(1-\theta^{-1}\big)-\frac{1}{c_{1}\theta}\frac{b}{k-b}\Big)=U\Big(k-b,-b,\frac{1}{c_{1}}\big(1-\theta^{-1}\big)\Big)\big(1+\mathcal{O}(1/\sqrt{k})\big),

so that the lower and upper bound match up to error terms. By (
7.36
), we thus arrive at

(7.41)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}{}

\displaystyle x^{k}(\theta-1+x)^{-k}c_{1}^{-1}(1-x)^{-(2+b)}\mathrm{e}^{-c_{1}^{-1}x/(1-x)}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{c_{1}^{-1}(1-\theta^{-1})/2}\sqrt{\pi}c_{1}^{-1/4+b/2}((1-\theta^{-1})k)^{1/4+b/2}\mathrm{e}^{-2\sqrt{c_{1}^{-1}(1-\theta^{-1})k}}\theta^{-k}\big(1+\mathcal{O}\big(1/\sqrt{k}\big)\big)

\displaystyle=Ck^{1/4+b/2}\mathrm{e}^{-2\sqrt{c_{1}^{-1}(1-\theta^{-1})k}}\theta^{-k}\big(1+\mathcal{O}\big(1/\sqrt{k}\big)\big).

Finally, when considering the second integral in (
7.27
), we observe its integrand is similar to that of the first integral but with a different constant in front and with a constant
\widetilde{b}=b-1
in the polynomial exponent. We can thus follow the exact same steps as for the first integral in (
7.27
) to conclude that it can be included in the
\mathcal{O}(1/\sqrt{k})
term in (
7.41
). We thus obtain that

(7.42)

p_{\geq k}=Ck^{1/4+b/2}\mathrm{e}^{-2\sqrt{c_{1}^{-1}(1-\theta^{-1})k}}\theta^{-k}\big(1+\mathcal{O}\big(1/\sqrt{k}\big)\big),

as required.

We now show the result for
p_{k}
, which uses the above steps with several minor adjustments. First,

(7.43)

\displaystyle p_{k}={}

\displaystyle(\theta-1)\int_{0}^{1}x^{k}(\theta-1+x)^{-(k+1)}c_{1}^{-1}(1-x)^{-(2+b)}\mathrm{e}^{-c_{1}^{-1}x/(1-x)}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle-(\theta-1)\int_{0}^{1}x^{k}(\theta-1+x)^{-(k+1)}b(1-x)^{-(b+1)}\mathrm{e}^{-c_{1}^{-1}x/(1-x)}\,\mathrm{d}x.

As for the proof of the asymptotic expression of
p_{\geq k}
, we consider the first integral only as the second one is of lower order. This yields

(7.44)

\displaystyle(\theta-1){}

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}x^{k}(\theta-1+x)^{-(k+1)}c_{1}^{-1}(1-x)^{-(2+b)}\mathrm{e}^{c_{1}^{-1}x/(1-x)}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle(1-\theta^{-1})c_{1}^{-1}\theta^{-k}\int_{0}^{\infty}u^{k}(1+u)^{b-k}\Big(1-\frac{1}{\theta(1+u)}\Big)^{-(k+1)}\mathrm{e}^{-c_{1}^{-1}u}\,\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle(1-\theta^{-1})c_{1}^{-1}\theta^{-k}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\binom{j+k}{j}\theta^{-j}\int_{0}^{\infty}u^{k}(1+u)^{b-(j+k)}\mathrm{e}^{-c_{1}^{-1}u}\,\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle(1-\theta^{-1})c_{1}^{-1}\theta^{-k}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\binom{j+k}{j}\theta^{-j}\Gamma(k+1)U(k+1,2+b-j,c_{1}^{-1})

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle(1-\theta^{-1})c_{1}^{b}\theta^{-k}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\binom{j+k}{j}(c_{1}\theta)^{-j}\Gamma(k+1)U(k+j-b,j-b,c_{1}^{-1})

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle(1-\theta^{-1})c_{1}^{b}\theta^{-k}\frac{\Gamma(k+1)}{\Gamma(k-b)}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{(k+1)^{(j)}}{(k-b)^{(j)}}\frac{(c_{1}\theta)^{-j}}{j!}\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!u^{k+j-b-1}(1+u)^{-(k+1)}\mathrm{e}^{-c_{1}^{-1}u}\,\mathrm{d}u.

Similar to (
7.33
), we bound

(7.45)

\frac{(k-1)^{(j)}}{(k-b)^{(j)}}\geq\begin{cases}1&\mbox{if }k>b\geq-1,\\
\Big(\frac{k+1}{k-b}\Big)^{j}&\mbox{if }b<-1.\end{cases}\quad\text{ and }\quad\frac{(k)^{(j)}}{(k-b)^{(j)}}\leq\begin{cases}1&\mbox{if }b<-1,\\
\Big(\frac{k+1}{k-b}\Big)^{j}&\mbox{if }k>b\geq-1.\end{cases}

Again, we assume without loss of generality that
b\geq-1
. Moreover, we only concern ourselves with the lower bound on
(k+1)^{(j)}/(k-b)^{(j)}
when
b\geq-1
, since we obtain a matching upper bound with the required error term when using the upper bound on
(k+1)^{(j)}/(k-b)^{(j)}
when
b\geq-1
, as in the proof for
p_{\geq k}
. Thus, we obtain the lower bound

(7.46)

\displaystyle(1-\theta^{-1}){}

\displaystyle c_{1}^{b}\theta^{-k}\frac{\Gamma(k+1)}{\Gamma(k-b)}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{(c_{1}\theta)^{-j}}{j!}\int_{0}^{\infty}u^{k+j-b-1}(1+u)^{-(k+1)}\mathrm{e}^{-c_{1}^{-1}u}\,\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle=(1-\theta^{-1})c_{1}^{b}k\theta^{-k}\frac{\Gamma(k)}{\Gamma(k-b)}\int_{0}^{\infty}u^{k+j-b-1}(1+u)^{-(k+1)}\mathrm{e}^{-c_{1}^{-1}(1-\theta^{-1})u}\,\mathrm{d}u,

which, up to the constant
(1-\theta^{-1})
, is the exact same expression as in (
7.34
). As discussed above, using the upper bound on
(k+1)^{(j)}/(k-b)^{(j)}
yields a matching upper bound (up to error terms), from which the result follows.
∎

Recall that in this example we set

(7.47)

\displaystyle X^{(n)}_{i}:={}

\displaystyle\big|\big\{j\in[n]:\mathcal{Z}_{n}(j)=\big\lfloor\log_{\theta}n-C_{\theta,1,c_{1}}\sqrt{\log_{\theta}n}+(b/2+1/4)\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n\big\rfloor+i\big\}\big|,

\displaystyle X^{(n)}_{\geq i}:={}

\displaystyle\big|\big\{j\in[n]:\mathcal{Z}_{n}(j)\geq\big\lfloor\log_{\theta}n-C_{\theta,1,c_{1}}\sqrt{\log_{\theta}n}+(b/2+1/4)\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n\big\rfloor+i\big\}\big|,

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{n}:={}

\displaystyle\big(\log_{\theta}n-C_{\theta,1,c_{1}}\sqrt{\log_{\theta}n}+(b/2+1/4)\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n\big)

\displaystyle-\big\lfloor\log_{\theta}n-C_{\theta,1,c_{1}}\sqrt{\log_{\theta}n}+(b/2+1/4)\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n\big\rfloor.

We then state the analogue of Proposition
5.6
.

Proposition 7.4
.

Consider the WRT model as in Definition
2.1
with vertex-weights
(W_{i})_{i\in[n]}
whose distribution satisfies (
4.9
) for some
b\in\mathbb{R},c_{1}>0
such that
bc_{1}\leq 1
, and recall
c_{c_{1},b,\theta}
from (
4.12
). For a fixed
K\in\mathbb{N},c\in(1,\theta/(\theta-1))
the following holds. For any integer-valued
i_{n},i_{n}^{\prime}
such that
0<\log_{\theta}n+i_{n}<\log_{\theta}n+i_{n}^{\prime}<c\log_{\theta}n
and
i_{n},i_{n}^{\prime}=\delta\sqrt{\log_{\theta}n}+o(\sqrt{\log n})
for some
\delta\in\mathbb{R}
and for
a_{i_{n}},\ldots,a_{i_{n}^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
satisfying
a_{i_{n}}+\ldots+a_{i_{n}^{\prime}}=K
,

(7.48)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg[\!\Big(X^{(n_{\ell})}_{\geq i_{n}^{\prime}}\Big)_{a_{i_{n}^{\prime}}}\!\prod_{k=i_{n}}^{i_{n}^{\prime}-1}\!\!\Big(X_{k}^{(n_{\ell})}\Big)_{a_{k}}\bigg]={}

\displaystyle\big(c_{c_{1},b,\theta}\theta^{-i_{n}^{\prime}+\varepsilon_{n}-C_{\theta,1,c_{1}}\delta/2}\big)^{a_{i_{n}^{\prime}}}\!\!\prod_{k=i_{n}}^{i_{n}^{\prime}-1}\!\!\big(c_{c_{1},b,\theta}(1-\theta^{-1})\theta^{-k+\varepsilon_{n}-C_{\theta,1,c_{1}}\delta/2}\big)^{a_{k}}

\displaystyle\times\Big(1+\mathcal{O}\Big(\frac{\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n}{\sqrt{\log_{\theta}n}}\vee\frac{|i_{n}-\sqrt{\log_{\theta}n}|\vee|i_{n}^{\prime}-\sqrt{\log_{\theta}n}|}{\sqrt{\log_{\theta}n}}\Big)\Big).

Proof.

Set
K^{\prime}:=K-a_{i_{n}^{\prime}}
and for each
i_{n}\leq k\leq i_{n}^{\prime}
and each
u
such that
\sum_{\ell=i_{n}}^{k-1}a_{\ell}<u\leq\sum_{\ell=i_{n}}^{k}a_{\ell}
, let
m_{u}=\big\lfloor\log_{\theta}n-C_{\theta,1,c_{1}}\sqrt{\log_{\theta}n}+(b/2+1/4)\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n\big\rfloor+k
. Also, let
(v_{u})_{u\in[K]}
be
K
vertices selected uniformly at random without replacement from
[n]
. Then, as the
X^{(n)}_{\geq k}
and
X^{(n)}_{k}
can be expressed as sums of indicators, using the same steps as in the proof of Proposition
5.6
,

(7.49)

\mathbb{E}\left[\Big(X^{(n)}_{\geq i_{n}^{\prime}}\Big)_{a_{i_{n}^{\prime}}}\prod_{k=i}^{i_{n}^{\prime}-1}\Big(X_{k}^{(n)}\Big)_{a_{k}}\right]=(n)_{K}\sum_{\ell=0}^{K^{\prime}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\subseteq[K^{\prime}]\\
|S|=\ell\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\ell}\mathbb{P}\!\left(\mathcal{Z}_{n}(v_{u})\geq m_{u}+\mathbbm{1}_{\{u\in S\}}\text{ for all }u\in[K]\right).

By Proposition
5.1
,

(7.50)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(\mathcal{Z}_{n}(v_{\ell})\geq m_{u}+\mathbbm{1}_{\{u\in S\}}\text{ for all }u\in[K]\right)=\prod_{u=1}^{K}\mathbb{E}\left[\Big(\frac{W}{\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]+W}\Big)^{m_{u}+\mathbbm{1}_{\{u\in S\}}}\right](1+o(n^{-\beta})),

for some
\beta>0
. By Lemma
7.3
(and recalling the constant
C
in (
4.12
)), when
|S|=\ell
,

(7.51)

\displaystyle\prod_{u=1}^{K}{}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\Big(\frac{W}{\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]+W}\Big)^{m_{u}+\mathbbm{1}_{\{u\in S\}}}\right]

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle(C)^{K}\theta^{-\sum_{u=1}^{K}m_{u}-\ell}\exp\Bigg(\!-2\sum_{u=1}^{K}\sqrt{\frac{1-\theta^{-1}}{c_{1}}\big(m_{u}+\mathbbm{1}_{\{u\in S\}}\big)}\Bigg)\prod_{u=1}^{K}(m_{u}+\mathbbm{1}_{\{u\in S\}})^{b/2+1/4}

\displaystyle\times(1+\mathcal{O}(1/\sqrt{\log n})).

Here, we are able to obtain the error term
1+\mathcal{O}(1/\sqrt{\log n})
due to the fact that
\log_{\theta}n+i_{n}>\eta\log n
for some
\eta>0
when
n
is large. We note that
C_{\theta,1,c_{1}}\log\theta=2\sqrt{c_{1}^{-1}(1-\theta^{-1})}
. As
i_{n},i_{n}^{\prime}\sim\delta\sqrt{\log_{\theta}n}
,

(7.52)

\prod_{u=1}^{K}(m_{u}+\mathbbm{1}_{\{u\in S\}})^{b/2+1/4}=(\log_{\theta}n)^{K(b/2+1/4)}\big(1+\mathcal{O}\big(1/\sqrt{\log_{\theta}n}\big)\big),

uniformly in
S
(and
\ell
). Moreover, again uniform in
S
and
\ell
,

(7.53)

\displaystyle\exp{}

\displaystyle\bigg(-C_{\theta,1,c_{1}}\log\theta\sum_{u=1}^{K}\sqrt{m_{u}+\mathbbm{1}_{\{u\in S\}}}\bigg)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\exp\Big(-\Big(C_{\theta,1,c_{1}}\log\theta\sqrt{\log_{\theta}n}-\frac{C_{\theta,1,c_{1}}-\delta}{2}\Big)\Big)^{K}

\displaystyle\times\Big(1+\mathcal{O}\Big(\frac{\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n}{\sqrt{\log_{\theta}n}}\vee\frac{|i_{n}-\sqrt{\log_{\theta}n}|\vee|i_{n}^{\prime}-\sqrt{\log_{\theta}n}|}{\sqrt{\log_{\theta}n}}\Big)\Big).

This last step follows from the fact that the first-order term of
m_{u}
is
\log_{\theta}n
and its second-order term is
-(C_{\theta,1,c_{1}}-\delta)\sqrt{\log_{\theta}n}
. Finally, its lower-order terms are
\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n+(|i-\sqrt{\log_{\theta}n}|\vee|i_{n}^{\prime}-\sqrt{\log_{\theta}n}|)
. Then, using a Taylor expansion for the square root yields the result. Combining all of the above and recalling that
c_{c_{1},b,\theta}=C\theta^{C_{\theta,1,c_{1}}^{2}/2}
, we thus arrive at

(7.54)

\displaystyle(n)_{K}{}

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=0}^{K^{\prime}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\subseteq[K^{\prime}]\\
|S|=\ell\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\ell}\Big(C(\log_{\theta}n)^{b/2+1/4}\exp\Big(-\Big(C_{\theta,1,c_{1}}\log\theta\Big(\sqrt{\log_{\theta}n}-\frac{C_{\theta,1,c_{1}}-\delta}{2}\Big)\Big)\Big)\Big)^{K}

\displaystyle\times\theta^{-\sum_{u=1}^{K}m_{u}-\ell}\Big(1+\mathcal{O}\Big(\frac{\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n}{\sqrt{\log_{\theta}n}}\vee\frac{|i_{n}-\sqrt{\log_{\theta}n}|\vee|i_{n}^{\prime}-\sqrt{\log_{\theta}n}|}{\sqrt{\log_{\theta}n}}\Big)\Big)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\big(c_{c_{1},b,\theta}\theta^{-i_{n}^{\prime}+\varepsilon_{n}-C_{\theta,1,c_{1}}\delta/2}\big)^{a_{i_{n}^{\prime}}}\prod_{k=i_{n}}^{i_{n}^{\prime}-1}\big(c_{c_{1},b,\theta}(1-\theta^{-1})\theta^{-k+\varepsilon_{n}-C_{\theta,1,c_{1}}\delta/2}\big)^{a_{k}}

\displaystyle\times\Big(1+\mathcal{O}\Big(\frac{\log_{\theta}\log_{\theta}n}{\sqrt{\log_{\theta}n}}\vee\frac{|i_{n}-\sqrt{\log_{\theta}n}|\vee|i_{n}^{\prime}-\sqrt{\log_{\theta}n}|}{\sqrt{\log_{\theta}n}}\Big)\Big),

where the last step follows from a similar argument as in the proof of Proposition
5.6
.
∎

With Proposition
7.4
at hand, the proofs of Theorems
4.6
,
4.7
, and
4.8
follow the same approach as the proofs of Theorems
2.5
,
2.8
, and
2.9
.
